Or from a heavy, tired feeling on rising in the morning?

Or are you nervous, dizzy with specks floating before your eyes?

Is your appetite poor, or are you losing weight?

Have you ever suspected your kidneys?

Week after week for years past. Warrington cases of kidney troubles, relieved by Doan's Backache Kidney Pills, have been reported in these columns, and it is only natural that Warrington should now be asking, "Have these local cures we read of, months ago, proved lasting cures? Are these neighbours of ours still well ? "

SHOOTING A RHINOCEROS.

As the popular natural history book says, the rhinoceros is a strange beast. He is. When a bullet hits a rhino, says a writer in the Cuuntry Gentleman, no one can say with certainty what he will do. With most animals one has a very fair idea as to what will happen; but with rhino, no. Some people go so far as to say that the rhino does not know himself. Sometimes he will spin round like a humming-top, but squeal- ing like a pig; suddenly off he will go straight as an arrow.

There seems to be no method in his madness, though, for when he does go he goes, or rather comes, straight for you. Then is the time for the tree, thorny though it be. At other times, when not knocked out of time by the first shot, he reminds one rather of an engine whose driver has turned on steam a bit too quickly, so that the wheels skid; this is simply his preliminary turn in your direction, and then, with as little, loss of time as the engine, down he comes at you, snorting and puffing rather more noisily. This is another case for the tree. Sometitnes there is no tree, any way not close enough, and then an anthill must do to .get behind; it is as well to have one or she other. At other times the beast will simply make off straight as an ordinary well-minded animal should. ATh the same it is this uncertainty that adds so greatly to the zest of the game.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
CHURCH SOCIETY.

Membership of 200 Deplored by Canon Stevenson.

CHURCH HOUSE CO-OPERATION.

The annual meeting of the Warr:ngton Church Scciety was hyld in the Church House, Bridge-street, on Tuesday evening,the Rector (the Rev. Canon Willis) presiding. There were also present : The Rev. G. Vale Owen (hon. secretary), Mr. C. A. Littler (hon. treasurer), the Revs. Canon Stevenson, B. Percival, J. H. Garnett, and W. Bracecamp, Messrs. J. H. Smethurst, C. S. Burgess, W. Milner, A. J. Pradshaw, R. Sumner, and A. F. R. Hughes. CO-OPERATION WITH THE CHURCH HOUSE. 'The SECRETARY read the 21st annual report, in which the committee thanked the lecturers and preachers who had given their help during the year. There was no anniversary service this year and the work opened with three lectures at St. Clement's Mission. After giving an interesting resume of the year's work the report continued that negotiations had been proceed- ing between that committee and the committee of the Warrington Church House, with a view to establishing a working arrangement which would enable the two societies to co-operate in church work. The proposals were: (1) That the Church Society members should have the privilege of joining the Church House at an annual subscription of 2s. 6d: instead of 10s. 6d., the ordinary subscription. (2) That the Church Society Day over to the Church House as large a sum as possible from their balarce left at the end of the season. (3) That three members of the Church Society Committee be co-opted on to the Church House Committee. It was hoped by thus drawing the two societies together to strengthen them both, making them better able to realise their ideal of being centres for the Church life of the town and neighbourhood.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
A PROTEST AGAINST NARROW PAROCHIALISM.

The Church Society continued to carry on ije gooi work year after year. By its autumn lectures it sought to educate its members on any phase of the Church's work, past or present, which was of interest to Church people. By its Lenten lectures. given by some of the most able and powerful preachers in the country, it Fought to build up the members of the Church in their most holy faith. The Society formed a nucleus round which, at any time, the whole strength of the Church in the district might unite to repel attack. Finally, its existence was a protest against the narrow parochialism, reminding them that the cause for which the Church stood was not parochial only, but world-wide. The balance-sheet showed that the year concluded wi,h a balance in hand of £l6 195., as against £26 15s. sd. the previous year. The membership of the society was 200. Mr. BURGESS, in proposing the adoption of the report and statement of accounts, said it was very important that they should bear in mind that the Church House should receive their full support. In allocating the money to be shared between the society and the Church House, he hoped the members would consider that the Church Society should receive the lion's share. Rev. B. PERCIVAL : I may have something to say about allocating.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
NOT REPRESENTATIVE OF CHURCH STRENGTH.

Canon STEVENSON said they had had a most excellent report. He thought, however, in face of the decrease in members that something might be done to bring the claims of the society before the Church people in the town. The work of the Church and the Church Society might form an appropriate subject for sermons. It seemed to him deplorable that in a town like Warrington there should only be 200 people who were members of a society such as that. It did not represent the strength of the Church in the town by any means. He hoped the anniversary services would be continued. The report was adopted and suggestions approved. OFFICERS ELECTED.

On the proposition of Mr. SMETHURST, seconded by the Rev. W. BRACECAMP, the Rector was unanimously re-elected president of the society. The Rev. G. Vale Owen and Mr. C. A. Littler were re-elected hon. uecretary and hon. treasurer respec- tively. When the election of the committee came before the meeting one member suggested that one or two ladies might be appointed, in reply to which the Rector jocularly remarked that they did not want any suffragettes. The committee will be :—The Revs. Canon Stevenson, W. Bracecamp, G. C. Brings, E. C. E. Carleton, J. Collins, H. H. Corsbie, L. J. T. Darwall, J. Davies, G. W. Dickinson, H. B. Firth, J. H. G. Hollingworth, B. Percival, H. V. Pigot, Messrs. C. S. Burgess, R. Done, J. Hesketh, J. Hill, 0. Lofthouse, J. H. Smethurst, T. S. Steel, Turton, and H. J. Westbrook. It was also decided that the contribution to the Church House should he £lO 10s. ; that arrangements be made for a special sermon in all the churches explaining the objects of the society; and that the committee consider the question of the anniversary services.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
HINTS FOR THE HOME.

NEURITIS.

In its real sense neuritis mears inflamms- 'Zion of a nerve or nerves, and is accompanied by great pain, especially at night. The muscles of the limb appear to be weakened, and the skin is slightly inflamed. Hot fomen- tations or poultices should be placed on the affected part, says a writer in Health. The bowels should be kept right and the food should be plain and nourishing but not over- stimulating. There is another form of neuri- tis which comes from the too frequent use of alcohol. The legs become tender, and appear to be slightly paralysed, and there is difficulty in raising the feet. In this case electrical ap- plications to the limbs and massage are useful.

COOKING AND SERVING ASPARAGUS. Instructions as to the cooking of asparagus as given in the generality of books of cookery provide for laying the stalks horizontally in a long pan and boiling for alcout twenty minutes. This system, writes Charles Cooper in the Queen, has the effect of. half cooking the stalk and overcooking the head, thus diminishing its flavour and consistence. A far better plan to be adopted with asparagus of the stouter sort is that practised by Sir Henry Thompson. The stalks should be cut in equal lengths and the asparagus should be stood upright in a deep saucepan, the heads being nearly two inches above water; they should be boiled from thirty to fifty minutes, the steam from the water sufficing to cook the tender heads, while the prolonged cooking renders about a third more of the stalk eat- able The custom of serving asparagus on a slice of toast over which is poured a bath of flour and water, called white sauce, has been unsparingly denounced by good authorities. The object of serving upon toast is that it should absorb the moisture, and not that the toast should be regarded as an addition to the dish. The same object of absorbing superfluous moisture is attained by laying the asparagus upon a clean napkin. Theodore Child pertinently remarks; "If you do find: asparagus served on toast do not offer to eat the toast, any more than you would offer to eat the napkin." Asparagus should be served: on a long dish, the stalks arranged longitu- dinally on the napkin, and the sauce should always be served separately.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
WOMEN'S QUESTIONS. Could Lady-Architects Improve Upon Our Man-made Houses? ALEXA-IDR—A DAY. 1

The custom copied from Vienna of selling, a particular flower on a certain day in aid of charity, which was so successful in London last year, is to be observed again, but on a much larger scale, on. June 25th. This year, nearly three hundred towns in the United Kingdom have organised similar sales, that in Warrington having been fixed for June 28th. It is said that three farthings of every penny taken, goes to charities, approved by Queen Alexandra. Despite the fact that her Majesty is in mourning for her brother, the late King of Greece, it is hoped that she will drive round the various centres as she did last year. On this occasion an additional spectacular feature will be provided by:a long procession of rose-decorated motor cars through the principal streets of the Metropolis. Prizes will be given, and the duties of judges have been undertaken by the Duchess of Sutherland, the Hon. Arthur Stanley, and Lady Wilmer Lawson. All the roses are made by crippled girls. The little pink and white emblems cost is. Bd. per gross to produce, which is a little more than the market price, but higher pay is given so as to avoid any suggestion of sweating the workers. The many millions of roses required have provided work for several hundred girls for the best part of a year. Many of these workers are not only cripples, but blind as well. The Countess of Milton is at the head of the organisation, and a great number of ladies, well known in society, are working with her. MAN-MADE HOUSES.

Women often complain tbat they have to live in inconvenient houses, built entirely and planned by men. In Denmark that is not so universally the case, and a Danish lady boasted recently, when on a visit to this country, that she lived in a house which, together with its furniture, was designed and planned and con- structed entirely by women. The profession of architecture has been more actively taken up by lady aspirants on the Continent than is the case in this country, although neither the Royal Institute of British Architects, nor the Society of Architects, has barred its doors to women. These two institutions muster only three lady associates between them. No doubt it is because women are not adapted physically to the outdoor portion of the work that there are so few

lady architects, but it appears that several are now studying, with a view to presenting themselves for the examinations necessary for admission to either of the societies. The male profession, of course, ridicules the idea that women architects would improve on man- made houses, but, as a rule, women certainly improve on the bachelor-arranged house. By altering and trying new effects, they contrive to make the internal arrangements much less monotonous than the "mere man" is content with, and the same ingenuity might with advantage be applied to the structural design of dwelling-houses. CLEANING THE FRONT DOORSTEPS. One of the difficulties which confronts the middle class housewife, left servantless, is the cleaning of the front doorsteps. If we had women architects, things might be managed differently, for it was left to a woman to solve this problem as far as she is con- cerned. This she did by having a connection made with the pipe which supplied hot water over the sink, and carried outside the front door, so as to throw a jet over the doorstep. Then by simply turning on the tap, she could do all the cleaning required without showing herself to neighbours or the public. It is a useful device which the architect, who thinks that man-made houses cannot be improved upon, might make a note of—for the doorstep girl, who used to go round the town, on Saturdays each week, offering her services to servantless mistresses, disappeared as a domestic institution with the close of the Victorian era. THE CHANGING SILHOUETTE. There is no manner of doubt that the extreme of slimness has been reached and the pendulum has been set in motion in the opposite direction. The change may be scarcely perceptible, but it has begun. Already flounces and tunics give width to skirts, as also those bodices that have fulness under the arms. Waists, too, are big. Sleeves full and long, with hanging lace, are seen on dresses made of soft thin materials, and the effect is not without a certain charm, but it is doubtful if they are to be recommended in heavier fabrics. Even gloves are much wrinkled, all of which changes have a significance for the bystander who notes the signs and portents of fashion. A new sleeve is of chiffon, shaped like a bishop's, and tied in at the wrist with velvet, a frill of chiffon falling over the wrist. A three-quarter sleeve of silk, or moil* or of the material of which the dress is composed, is worn over it. Another model had sleeves of the kimono type, hanging in ample folds to the elbow, below which it was caught into a band. All the sleeves were made in the lightest of materials. THE CHANGE IN DRAPERIES. It is satisfactory to note in beautiful gowns that, despite the fact that they cannot be termed simple, there is a general absence of bewildering effects in draperies, that demand a statuesque repose in their wearers if the grotesque is to be avoided. Tunics and aprons, ribbon bands and garlands of flowers take the place of draperies, the bands and garlands being veiled with flounces, or thin folds of some flimsy material. A startling innovation at Long- champs was the decolletee dress, worn by a goodly number of the most beautifully dressed women, the lightest of tulle draperies or scarves being arranged on the shoulders and falling to the knees. There is no likelihood that evening dress will be worn at our race meetings, as the real gentlewoman possesses too much reserve to appear thus apparelled by daylight, and out-of-doors. BEADED CLOAKS. Although the long coat and cloak for evening wear are still popular, there is no doubt that the three- quarter length for these garments is coming to the fore. The vogue for beads—which is nothing short of a craze—has been extended to these confections intended for race meetings cr smart receptions, or which may also be worn for evening. Bugles and jet seem more fitted for the elderly than the young, but so bewitching are these beaded coats and cloaks, that they appeal to women of all ages. Jet, crystal, steel, silver and gold, all combine to enhance the sheen of the satin on which are worked bold conventional designs that remind one of the wall-paper patterns of some years ago. It is simply astonishing to note the effects that can be obtained by means of beads. A soft rose satin coat is covered with masses of crystal globules, and the opalescent effect is lovely. Another garment is very striking; a twilight sky being pro- duced by the use of pale blue satin and moonlight beads, then from steel and silver through tints of grey, the design is teautifully ehaded to the white of a soft cloud.

Children's Food.

GUARDIANS' POINTED CRITICISM.

The cost of the provisions given to the children at the Padgate Cottage Homes, which works out at a little over fourpence per head per day, has given rise to a suggestion by the assistant district auditor that the amount s unusually high for such an institution. The statement called forth brief. but pointed, comments at the meeting of the Board of Guardians on Wednesday. In the absence of the Chairman (Mr. E. L. Bent), Mr. A. J. Jolley presided. There were also present: Mrs. Simpson, Mrs. Hall, Rev. A. M. Mitchell, Messrs. G. Mather, W. C. Brookes, R. Lee, W. Furness, Philip C. Garnett, F. McKinley, W. Flanagan, T. Dale, J. A. Hughes. A. Bottomley (clerk), R. I. Partridge (assistant clerk), W. J. Brown (master), and C. E. Lawrence (superintendent of Cottage Homes). ASSISTANT MASTER'S SALARY. On the proposition of Mr. %V. C. BROOKS, seconded by Mr. LEE, the Board approved a resolution increasing the salary of Mr. 0. A. Moss, assistant master, from £l5 to £5O per annum, with emoluments as heretofore. ASSISTANT AUDITOR'S CRITICISMS. The Local Government Board transmitted for the consideration of the Board an extract from a report dated April 11th, 1913, by Mr. P. T. Turner, assistant district auditor, after his audit of the accounts of the Union Workhouse for the half-year ended 30th September, 1912. The report stated that stocks of clothing and materials were excessive. That was due to the system adopted by the Guardians of purchasing all articles estimated to be required—without regard to the actual requirements of the institution. The following were some examples of excess stocks:—Drill, purchased in half-year, 966 yards ; consumed in half- year, 113 yards; remaining in store. 986 yards. White flannel, purchased nil ; consumed, 88 yards ; remaining in store 294 yards. Hessian,:purchased, 102 yards; consumed nil ; remaining in store, 202 yards. THE COST OF FOOD AT PADGATE. A similar report was presented with regard to the Cottage Homes for the same half-year. it was added that no returns to store appeared to be made by the various foster mothers, who drew from stores the full scale rations for children under their care. The cost of provisions per head per day was 434 pence, which was high for an institution of that character. The CHAIRMAN characterised the assistant auditor's reference to the cost of food given to the Padgate children as a piece of impertinence. It would be interesting to know whether the assistant auditor's food cost as much as fourpence a day. Mr. MCKINLEY : I would not like my children to live on that much a day. The Rev. A. M. MITCHELL thought the question of the amount of stock was a serious one, as the assistant auditor's comments seemed to cast some reflection upon the management of the institutions. He admitted there might be some explanation to put a different colour upon it, but the question ought to be gone into in detail. The CLERK: I would like the Board distinctly to understand that the report does not refer to our present officers, in either case. It relates to the half year ended September 30th, 1912. The Rev. A. M. MITCHELL : That gives us the opportunity of going into the matter more thoroughly. Mr. GARNETT suggested that they should take stock with the object of ascertaining the present state of the stores. The CHAIRMAN said that, after the opinions which had been expressed—none too strongly—about the misdirected energies of the assistant auditor in a certain direction, it would be well to refer the reports to the House Committee and the Cottage Homes Com- mittee respectively. This was agreed to.

MR. CROSFIELD AND THE SUFFRAGE QUESTION.

Sir,- -Mr. Crcsfield is very often referred to in your columns as the "Progressive" candidate. Is he yet sufficiently progressive to be a believer and supporter of "Votes for Women " ? Or does he, like the Prime Minister, believe that "Taxation (of both sexes) with- out representation (of one sex) is tyranny." As one who believes that the breaking down of the sex barrier is the very first step that a party, laying claim to be a democratic party should take, I note with great satisfaction that the finest, most independent spirits among the Liberal women are leaving the party ranks until they receive proper treatment at the hands of a;Liberal Government. Mrs. Stewart-Brown has this week resigned her presidency of the Lance- shire'and Cheshire Women Liberal Associations. Many others ha I already ceased to work in support of Liberals as a party. I was delighted to see a letter from Miss Crosfield in your columns a few weeks ago, headed, I think, "Party before Principle," regarding the supporting of Liberal candidates who were not avowed supporters of the extension of the franchise to women. Unless Mr. Crosfield, before the next election, definitely decides that the enfranchisement of women is one, if not the move for a Liberal Cabinet to make, I hope to see Mie,s Crosfield, as well as most of the other Liberal women, leaving him to fight the election without any help from the (politically) inferior sex. Whilst giving place to no one in my admiration of Mr. Crosfield's political work (carried on far away from the glare of the " footlights "), I deplore that he should not, long before this, have realised, as the people of other countries are realising far more rapidly than we are, that the cry of" Government by the people for the people " is utterly unreasonable when the party adopting that cry deliberately,and even mockingly, excludes the majority of the people from any part in the said Government. I am strongly of opinion that the fine work done by the Liberal Government during the last five or six years has been almost entirely nullified by the treat- ment given to the Women'sßills in Parliament and to their supporta!' outside. How does Mr. Crosfield stand to-day on this question?

J. S. TILLING.

Bewsey-street, Warrington. June 19th, 1913.

THE TARIFF QUESTION IN THE UNITED STATES.

_ Sir, —I notice that some of your correspondents and several local political speakers are continually falling into error over the question of the tariffs of the United States. They state that the United States are anxious for Free Trade and assume that the present version of the tariff there is on that basis. They are evidently not familiar with the political history of the State; otherwise they would know that the tariffs are simply periodically lowered or highered accord- ing to the economic position and the judgment cf the political parties. Dr. Wilson, the new President of the States, whom some people unwittingly claim as a Free Trader, on speaking of the matter and declaring the policy of the Democratic party in September, 1912, put it clearly when he said: "Do you suppose half the population of the United States is going to destroy the very fonndation a our economic life by running amok among the tariff schedules? That is not our programme, and anybody who says it is simply does not understand the situa- tion in the United States at all. There should be no fear of the Free Trade bugaboo, because the Democrats do not propose to revise the tariff in any other way than will conserve the real business interests of the country. We propose to revise those schedules which give unfair private advantage to certain interests without affecting business which is sound and legitimate, and which we all wish to see promoted. Neither I nor the Democratic party stands for Free Trade." - - .

_ As the other great political party of the United States, viz., the Republican party, are pronounced Protectionists, the hopes of some people on this side, that the United States will go in for Free Trade, rests upon a very flimsy foundation. The late Abraham Lincoln, the great American patriot, in a discussion on the same matter, said: "I do not know much about the tariff, but I do know this much : When we buy the goods abroad we get the goods and the foreigner gets the money, but when we buy the goods made at home,we get both the goods and the money." Lincoln, if he didn't know much about the tariff, certainly was clear in his own mind, like Dr. Wilson, that it was sounder economics to encourage and foster the home market. E. ENGLAND.

LORD'S DAY OBSERVANCE ACT AND LIBERTY. Sir,-1 should like to say a few words about the action of the ministry and others in regard to Sunday closing. The Act, which will be brought into opera- tion, should steps be taken to enforce Sunday closing, is at the present time quite obsolete. The Lord's Day Observance Act of Charles 11., 1677, forbids the public crying of goods on Sunday, and prohibits any tradesman, workman, artificer, or labourer, working , on Sunday, using any boat or barge, or exposing any goods for sale, except mackerel, which may be sold before and after Divine Service, and milk, which may be sold before 9 a.m. and after 4 p.m. ; Jews being exempted. Physicians, solicitors, farmers, proprietors, land drivers of hackney carriages have also been held to be legally exempt. A later Act of George 111. (1780) prohibits any place being opened for public entertainment or for debating on any subject on Sundays, admission to which is by money or ticket. These two Acts were framed by persons who believed that Sunday was by divine authority set apart as" The Lord's Day," on which it was considered religiously wrong to do any work. They were, therefore, passed under what it now known and proved to be an error. The Act of Charles 11. had become practically obsolete till, in 1870-71, an attempt „Fes made by a Sunday Observance Society to revife it by prosecu- tions, which were, however, considered by the public harsh and unjust. An Amendment Act. therefore,was passed in 1871 (34 and 35 Viet.), which enacted that no proceeding should be instituted without the consent of the chief officer of the police of the district, or of two Justices of the Peace, or of a stipendiary magis- trate. The Act of George 111. was also amended in 1875 by "The Remission of Penalties Act," which invests the King with power to remit any fine imposed by the former Act. But the two objectionable Acts still remain as examples of the stupid ignorance and religious intolerance of a past generation. These Acts are objectionable for the following reasons : (1) U here is no divinely-appointed "Lord's Day" under the Christian dispensation, so that for years the people of this country have been robbed of one day out of every week—one year out of every seven—for an imaginary religious principle which never existed, except in the minds of a handful of intolerant:religious fanatics. "The great writers to whom the world owes what religious liberty it possesses," said the late John Stuart Mill, " have mostly asserted freedom of conscience to be an indefeasible right, and denied absolutely that a human being is accountable to others for his religious belief." Religious belief varies geographically, and is chiefly dependent on the, acci- dent of birth. (2) Though the Jews are exempt from the Act of Charles IL. no exception is made as regards those outside the pale of orthodoxy—Unitarians, Free_ thinkers, Rationalists, Agnostics, &c. ; nor as regards the large number of nominal Christians, all of whom, if canvassed, would strongly resent the suspension of their liberty on one day of the week, which these Acii

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

UNCLE'S HEROISM. DIVED INTO DISUSED WELL IN ATTEMPT TO SAVE NEPHEW. .

An inquest was held at Farnworth,Widnes,cn Monday relating to the death of Herbert Harrison, aged 13, son of flemry Harrison, chemical labourer, of Viaduct- street, Widnes. The boy went to his uncle's house on Friday after- noon to bring his younger brother home. They after- wards strayed towards a disused well in a field off Moorfleld-road. Herbert was in the act of throwing into the well when he fell in. His uncle, William flacon, who was attracted to the spot by cries, dived into the water, bat failed to resell() the boy. The uncle had a narrow escape from death, due to exhaustion. There was a drop of three feet down the brick side before the water was reached. Seeing the predicament the rescuer was in, George Barnet loaned over the edge of the well, whilst another man held his feet, and in this way Raton was pulled out. The body of the boy was recovered later. In answer to Coroner Brighouse, Hutton said he could swim, but it was the first time he had Iresn in the water with his clothes on, and the weight of these was gradually pulling him down. The jury returned a verdict of "Accidental death," and complimented Hufton. The Coroner said his conduct would be brought before the notice of the Humane Society.

A WANDERER'S LIFE. A SAD STORY TOLD AT THE RUNCORN POLICE COURT.

- - --- A strange story was told at Runcorn on Saturday, when Benjamin Harris and Nellie Heath, who were described as tramps, appeared before the magistrates. Detective Davies found the prisoners begging at shops in Regent-street and Church-street. Inquiries had been made, said Superintendent Ennion, and it was found that the female prisoner was a native of Bloodford-street, Crewe. She was only twenty-two years of age, and had commenced the life of a wanderer since she was sixteen years of age. She had been in various lodging-houses in the country, and had been three years an inmate of Chester Asylum. The male prisoner was a married man with a family. He was a native of Rawtenstall. The evidence showed that prisoners had wandered during the week from Monmouthshire, and had visited Wrexham and Chester, and had slept in a brickkiln the night previous to their arrest. The woman's story about the man being her husband was a fabrication. The officer (recalled) told the Bench that what the man was doing when be saw him w!ts known as "keep- ing nix" among the tramp community. A sentence of fourteen days' hard labour was imposed on each prisoner.

ST. HELENS.

ST. HELENS TOWN HALL FIRE. WATCH COMMITTEE'S INQUIRY. On Monday a special inquiry was held by tho St. Helens Watch Committee as to the cause of the fire at the Town Hall, which caused from £lO,OOO to £20,000 damage, a week ago. A considerable number of witnesses were called, and eventually the Committee passed a resolution stating that in tho opinion of this Committee the fire was accidentally caused by work- men working with a blow lamp on a scaffold round the spire.

LABOUR PARTY AND ST. lIELENS. MR. JAMES SEXTON THE PROBABLE CANDIDATE.

---- It is expected that Mr. James Sexton will be the Labour candidate for St. Helens at the next election, in opposition to Mr. Rigby Swift, K.C., who was elected by 264 votes at the last general election. Mr. Swift was then opposed by the late Mr. Thomas Clover,• the treasurer of the Lancashire and Cheshire Miners' Federation, who had held the seat since the previous election in 1906. Few men are better known in the Labour world than, " Jimmy" Soxton, the leader of the Liverpool dockers who has for years been one of the most picturebque and 'striking figures at the annual gatherings of the Trade Union Congress, Interest will be added to the contest by the fact that both the candidates are Liverpool men.

LOVE, RELIGION, AND SUICIDE. A verdict of " Suicide while of unsound mind " was returned at an inquest on Monday, on Lavinia BiHinge, twenty, who was found drowned in the St. Helens Canal on Sunday. Deceased's mother, who lives at 191, Newton-road, St. Helens,said the girl had not lived at home for some time, and she had only seen her two or three times during the past fifteen months. She saw her on Saturday evening leaving the theatre with her sister, but did not speak to her. The deceased had been a great deal of trouble to them, and had often quarrelled with witness about turning over her wages,and when in a temper Ole had frequently threatened to drown herself. James Pimblett, collier, 21, South John-street, St, Helens, said that he kept company with the deceased up to the coal strike, about fifteen months ago. She then went to service in Liverpool, and they quarrelled on the matter of religion. On Saturday night he accidentally met her near the railway station, and she walked home with him, and asked him why be did not answer her letters that she had written to him from Liverpool. He told her that it was best that they should net correspond, as they would never agree on account of the difference in religion. She then said, "Good-night, Jim. It is the last time you will see me in £t,. Helens." Next day he body was found in the water,

The Examiner, Saturday, Sune 21, 1913. MORE TEACHERS WANTED.

Canon Stevenson on the Shortage of Training College Students. GOOD OPENINGS FOR GIRLS.

The educational world is confronted with a shortage in the supply of elementary school teachers and the difficulties thus created were alluded to by the Rev. Canon Stevenson, principal of the Warrington Train- ing College, in a speech at the animal prize-giving held at the college on Saturday afternoon. About 100 relatives and friends of the students were present, atid the prizes wore distributed by Mr. 11. Clialoner Dotvdall, Chancellor of the diocese of Liverpool. There were on the platform : The Ven. Archdeacon Spooner, and the Revs Canons Willis, Penrhyn and N unn.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0046.txt | included=true | included =====
FALLING OFF IN CANDIDATES.

The PRINCIPAL emphasised the fact to which he called attention last year—that there bad been a very serious falling off in the number of young people entering the profession of teachers in the elementaiy schools. In round numbers, the decrease during the last five or six years had Leen 50 per cent. We were already beginning to feel the pinch, for, according to the latest Government report, the cry was going up from many parts of the country that teachers were becoming so scarce that it was difficult for authorities to stall their schools. At the present moment the profession of a teacher in an elementary school, especially for girls, was A very excellent one. 'There were plenty of posts available, although he did not say that they couid always send a girt exactly where she wanted to go. She could, however, always be put somowhere—(laughter)—where she would be wry useful end get a reasonable income. The profession offered a very good opening, a steady income beginning with at least £7O a year and rising to something like .1:120, with the valuable addition recently made of en increase in the retiring pensi ,n which brought it up to about £5O a year. From his long experience of young ladies, be reg,ar:ie-1 the latter provision as very important indeed. Girls were much too soI t-hearted— (laughter)--and their tendency was to give away chat they could to a brother, a cousin or a friend, and not to put by fur old age, Nvlach seemed so extremely remote. tione the less, it crept upon them with speedy foot. Therefore, he rej,.iced in the fact that there was now something certain to be looked forward to by those who continued to teach limit the age of sixty-five. (Laughter.) lie knew it seemed a long time, and that eventualities might occur before it was reached, but a great many old students from that college had reached it and had been very glad to find that there was something to be had at that time.

FEnit STUDENTS—MORE COLLEGES. Side by side with the great falling off in the number of entrants into the profession there had been a large increase in the building of training c flleges. They had now come to the pass that there were not enough students to go round. At the present time some of the colleges were not full, and, in the course of a year or two, a great many of them would probably be in that positioo. The Warriugt-n College was lull, but they could not look forward to the next two or three years—by which time they hoped the tide would turn— without a certain amount of auxiety. The new colleges which were, generally speaking, municipal collegee, had behind them the purse of the ratepayer, but the old colleges, like that one, bad not, which made a great deal of difference. Ifs new college was not full—as some were not at the present time—the only person who suffered was the ratepayer, and they all know that nobody cared about him. (Laughter.) But if the old colleges, which were not upon the rates, were not full, it meant a very serious financial loss to them. The Canon went on to urge those presaut to recommend the advantage of the Warrington Training College to pro- spective candidates for tire profession.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0047.txt | included=true | included =====
A SUCCESSFUL YEAR.

Referring to the recent history of the cAlege, the Principal said they had had a very happy year. Their work bad gone on steaiily and well, and the reporta of the various examining bodies had bean excellent. Altogether the college was very prosperous and succw,s- ful in its working. (Applause.) He wished to express the great debt he owed to the efficient, able end loyal band of teachers who helped him in the conduot of the college. (ApplauEe.) "INO2 AS PROFITABLE AS SLXVE-DRIVING

===== 0002601_19130621_art0048.txt | included=true | included =====
OR SWINDLING."

Mr. CHALONER DOWDALL congratulated the 'students on their choico of a profes,ion. Of course, they could not expect that a profession, which was so high and honourable as to attiact people in all civilised countries to gise their services to it for nothing,to be tin incially so profitabls as slave-driving or swindling, or, indeed, as any ordinary form of honest and remunerative busi- ness. The occupation was is itself so attractive that naturally it was not lucrative. No one expected an arjet to make a: much money as a businessman. His mind was occupied and rej deed in his occupation,part from the profit that Caine from it. At the same time, as things went, a commencing salary of £7O a year, rising to £l2O, with a pension of £5O a year and the possibility of a headship, was not, in these days, an unattractive proposition. It was, hiwever, the other aspects of teaching which properly attracted people to it. To begin with, there wt:s the certainty of employ- ment, there was no risk. Ile remembered some time ago, when he represented a ward in the Liverpdol City Council, receiving on indignant letter from a psrent, saying, " 1 have spent money on the education of my child, and, unless you get her employment in a school, I shall vote against you and do everything 1 can to turn you out." (Laughter.) That, perhaps, was not the way to approach one, and be did not know that the indignant parent derived any profit from the com- munication. But, at all events, it recalled a state of things that was now past. It was quite certain that any girl leaving that college was sure of employment under conditions which reLeved the mind from anxiety. The salary was not euormuus, but it was sufficient to leave the mind free. People who embarked upon other enterprises were perpetually confronted with the daily conflict of life against their fellows in the matter of money. In the teaching profession, however, the salary was secure and the mind was free for the noblest Of occupations. The work was in itself dign,fied and worth doing.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0049.txt | included=true | included =====
"THESE MATERIALISTIC DAYS."

In these materialistic days people were losing faith and hope in everything, but so long as there was any shred of faith, hope or interest in any human being in this world, so long would be recognised the fact that the teaching of children was a thing worth doing. It was not one of those matters of which one could say, "Why are we doing it?" The good of it was so apparent and it appealed to any noble and generous nature. The children passed through the schools and no one knew the fruit that might come from what was said or felt by the teachers, and still more from the character of the teachers themselves. Their work was more permanent than the most stable ktructure that one could conceive; more enduring than the Pyramids. Life went °nand the endurance of ideas was one of the obvious facts of life. SOMETHING THAT MONEY CANNO r BUY.

lie congratulated the students,not only on their choice of profession, but on their choice of college. They were told that colleges such as that had to compete with municipal colleges which derived help from the rates, and therefore had a long purse at the back of them. Ihere was, however, something to be got in that college, which money could not buy. There were the tradition, the teaching and the manners of the place. Manners were not bought with money. They were the sweetening things of life and came from th,e deep, ultimate facts of charaz.;ter, and from the ideas by which character was to be controlled. Finally, he congratu- lated them on thoir Principal. (Applause.) They knew him and whet he did, and, what was more important, they knew what he was. (Applause.) He (the speaker) could open no wider or more ,t opeful • prJspect to the studonts who were going into the teaching profession than the fact that they could be to others what he had been to them. It was in that personal way that the most valuable things in life ware transmitted, and they left that college with that great benefit. (Applause.)

THE PRINCIPAL'S INFLUENCE.

The Rev. Canon PENRIEYN, in proposing a vote of thanks to Mr. Chaloner Dowdall, mentioned that he (the Cation) had attended alm)st every prize distribu- tion at the college, during the twenty-three years he had been at Winvvick,aud on each occasion he heard the same story of success. They felt year by year that the debt they owed him was an increasing one. It was impossible for anyone, even for those who were working, in some sense, behind the scenes, to gauge the tremendous effect of his influence, not only directly upon the happy students of the college, but indirectly throughout the length and breadth of this and other dioceses. He not only took an individualistic interest in every utudent cc rantitted to his care, hut had a broad outlook with regard to educational matters, and so had secured the confidence of headquarters in London The governors looked up to him 39 an expert, from many points of view, end any opinion which he expressed on the different problems which agitated the educational world was always to be received with attention and profit, The great success which had attended the ;liege was largely due to his inspiration, efficient control and admirable teaching, letcl..cd up most splendidly by the staff he had gathered around him, and whom he had attached to himself by the warmest possible ties. FARM AND GARDEN,

STRIKE THREATENED.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0052.txt | included=true | included =====
Notices Handed in by Three Thousand

Labourers.

TO CEASE WORK TO-DAY.

Notices by over two thousand West Lancashire farm labourers to cease work (t,o-day) were tendered on Saturday. Farmers between Liverpool and Ormsliirk on receipt of the notices promised their men increases in wages and one o'clock stop on Saturdays—two of the most important demands of the Agricultural Labourers' Union ; but there are others who state that they will firmly adhere to the previous decision of the farmers' meeting at Ormskirk to c2ase work at two o'clock on Saturday and treat with the men individu- ally regarding tho raising of wages. This the men and the union leaders state emphatically does not meet the requirements. They insist on one o'clock finish and collective bargaining, and if the strike takes place these will be the main issues. Hay crops and early potatoes are maturing rapidly under the present ideal weather conditiens, and if labour is not available serious difficulties will be experienced at harvest. A weak ago it was thought by farmers that the older men would not tender notices, but they are as enthusiastic as the younger labourers. Nor can the masters rely on Irish harvesters, three hundred of whom landed at Liver- pool on Saturday, for they declare that they are join- ing the Labourers' Union. The text of the notice is :—" We, the undersign -d, beg respectfully to give you notice that we require a 12-hour working day, with two hours off for meals, and 6d. an hour overtime for all hours worked longer than that time, and a minimum wage of 243. a week, with all allowances as at present, and to cease work on Saturdays at one o'clock p.m., this arrangement to commence on June 21et, 1913." _

===== 0002601_19130621_art0053.txt | included=true | included =====
SYMPATHY OF TRANSPORT WORKERS.

The farm labourers have been promised support, in the event of a strike, by the Liverpool Transport workers, who have offered to refuse to unload or handle arty farm produce &lining from the district, and not under the charge of union farmsmen. The transpprt workers are strongly in favour of the recognition of the union being made an essential part of any settlement between the farmers and their men, and, because 'they are such powerful allies, they are not without a good deal of influence with the farm workers.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0054.txt | included=true | included =====
HAYMAKING AND PLANT GROWTH.

Haymaking, says the "Times," was the principal operation last week in the southern counties. There were many fine fields of clover and mixed seeds in swath by the middle of the week, and in the absenc of appreciable rain drying proceeded rapidly, the keen winds making up for any deficiency in the power of the sun. The weather suited haymaking better than the growth of crops, the wind being too cold and the night temperatures too low for vegetation to make seasonable progress. There should be no standing still in plant growth in June and things could make little headway against the harsh winds of the past week. ,‘Stagnation out of season brings in its train a multitude of evils to emphasise the consequences of suspended activity. Blight and fungus enemies in their many forms are as3ociated with and assisted by atmospheric conditions of the kind common last week. The bet exp2ctationB have not been fulfilled, the second ~ week of Juno having failed to realise the promises of the first week. There is but one note, and that a favourable one, Concerning the rotation hay, but reports upon the condition of the other crops vary. We hear from Norfolk that the great barley crop of that county is thin, the plant having failed to tiller freely enough to fill the ground, while .wheat though good in parts is of moderato colour in others, and oats are variable. In the Up per Thames Valley wheat is approaching the caring stage, but is of no great length, and unless warmer weather comes soon .the ear will be no better than the stem. Potatoes and wangels have stood the trials well and growers are hopeful as to the result, although weeds are unduly plentiful and labour to keep them under difficult to procure. The sowing of swedes and turnips makes good progress in land freer from weeds than that in which the earlier green crops were planted. Meadow hay is backward and still an uncertain quantity, and the pastures are bare or productive according to the amount of stocking, the character of the land, and the quality of the manage- ment, judicious manuring having shown a wonderful influence upon the character as well as upon the quantity of the herb.)ge.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0055.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE GARDEN.

DISAPPOINTING FRUIT CROP.—According to the monthly report just published by the Board of Agricul- ;tire, the prospects for fruit during the present year are of a variable character. Strawberries, raspberries, and currants promise to be good ; but gooseberries, though good in some districts, are bad in others. Pears, especially in the south-eastern districts, are below the average, while apples promise a large crop. Plums and cherries are about normal. In many instances that have come under our own observation, says "The Garden," the last two fruits named failed badly in stoning. Since- the last week in May, says a " Times " corres- pondent, a great change for the worse has taken place. In some diAriets gooseberries dropped or were blown ( IT the bushes to a great extent. There has also been some dropping of black and red currants in many orchards. The crop of each kind is now gener- ally set at about two-thirds of an average. From all the principal fruit districts reports indicate an almost complete failure of pears. The plum crop is one of the smallest ever crown. The apple crop is a partial one. The aphis blight on plums and apples, and pat ticularly on the former, is one of the worst ever known, in spite of repeated sprayings in many cases. This and brown rot havo had much to do with the fruiting failure: but the main and general causes are believed to be the wet summer of last year, followed by a wet autumn, winter, and spring up to the middle of May, and the cold and wet weather of the blossoming periods. A GLORIOUS GARDEN PINK. —Few hardy plants that have been introduced during recent years have had such a warm reception as pink gloriosa. This has all the good attributes of the old garden pinks, and in addition gives us large, bold flowers of bright rose pink colour. Moreover, it remains in flower for several weeks, and in that respect is a great advance over old varieties. The flowers are as large as those of Mrs. Sinkins, but the calyces do not split as they do in that well-known variety, while, owing to the colour, it is far more effective in the garden. But its crowning glory is its scent, which has captivated every visitor to our garden during the last three weeks.—" The Garden."

DEATH OF NOTED CHESHIRE CHEESEMA KER.

The funeral took place on Wednesday at Aidford Churchyard, of Mrs. Mary Mullock, widow ci the late , Mr. Richard Mullock, of Poulton Hall, PuHord, who died at the home of one of her daughters, at High Blymhill, Salop, on Sunday, at the advanced age of eighty-five years. Mrs. Mullock had the honour in 1897 cf forwarding to Queen Victoria, at Windsor Castle, a specimen of her prize Cheshire cheese, which her Majesty had gt aciously consented to receive cut of compliment to the maker and to the late Duke of Westmiubter. Mrs. Mullock was a noted cheese prize-winner at all the leading shows in the county. WOMEN LIBERALS.

President of Lancashire and Cheshire Union Resigns.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0062.txt | included=true | included =====
The Suffrage Question,

-- Afro. Egerton Stewart-Brown has addressed the following letter to the Women's Liberal Associations affiliated to the Lancashire and Cheshire Union of Women's Liberal Associations :- 10, Ullet-road, Liverpool, S., June, 1913. I beg to inform you that I am resigning the office of Pfesident of the Lancashire and Cheshire Union of Women's Liberal Associations, which I have held since 1908, in succession to Miss Ashton, who found herself then, as I do now, out of sympathy with many of the affiliated associations with regard to their attitude on women's suffrage. I cannot remain president of an. organisation of Liberal women, some of whom even now continue to work for anti-suffrage candidates, as I feel that by so doing they are retarding the advance of true Liberalism as well as the progress of women's suffrage, which I lave always regarded as an essential part of Liberalism. The recent division in the House of Commons on the second reading of Mr. Dickenson's Bill for the Parlia- mentary Enfranchisement of Women showed :-- 1. That the number of Liberals who voted for the Bill far exceeded that of any other party. 2. That two-thirds of the Liberals who voted were in favour of women's suffrage. These facts seem to show that if Liberal women la ill now tal e up a firmer attitude than they have hitherto done on women's suffrage—and especially if they will refuse to support any Liberal candidate who is an anti-suffragist—we can shortly get this reform adopted as part of the Liberal programme. I shall continue to speak and work for Liberalism and to attain this end ; and my resignation does not mean that I am severing my connection with the party for which I have worked hard and unceasingly for 25 years. But I think I can now serve it better "in a position of greater freedom and less responsi- bility." Thanking you for your support during the past five years, rind hoping still to come amongst you and plead for the enfranchisement of women, which I con,ider to be the greatest of all reforms at the present moment.—l remain, S:c., NESSI E STEW ART-BROWN.

WARRINGTON ASSOCIATION'S REGRET. Mrs. Osborne, hon. secretary of the Warrin gtcn Women's Liberal Association, has forwarded to Mrs. Stewart-Brown the following resolution, on behalf of the committee of the association: " The Warrington Women's Liberal Association unanimously wish to express their regret at the announcement of your retirement from the presidency of the Lanca- shire and Cheshire Union. We have all not only an official but a personal recollection and admiration of your capable leadership, and we feel that your resignation will be very deeply felt in both counties. At the same time we are delighted to hear you will still continue to work amongst us, and trust that we may have the pleasure of welcoming you in Warri ng- ton at no distant date."

===== 0002601_19130621_art0063.txt | included=true | included =====
LORD WOLMER.

"Let Us Have a Trade Union For The

Empire."

Lord \Conner, M.P., attended on Saturday the annual demonstration of the Newton Division Conservative and Unionist Association in Garswood Park. An open- air meeting was held, with Mr. Arthur Smith in the

chair. - Lord Wolmer devoted cOlllO attention to a poster which, he said, had been put up in the constituency charging him with an attempt to injure trade unions. He said he withdrew nothing he had laid. fie bad always insisted that every trade unionist, whether he was a Liberal, a Socialist, or a Tory, had a right to his own political opinions, and he said that the men whose actions would injure the trade unions of this country were those who were trying to make the onions simply an adjunct of the Labour party. (Cheers.) He would always protest against any man being made to pay for political opinions with which he did not agree. He would say to every work- ing man friend of his, " Join your union by all meats, butll would rather see my right band cut of/ than pay a singe halfpenny towards the funds of my political opponents." (Hear, hear.) It their opponents were to fight on that question at the next election they were ready to meet them. "We say to the great trade unions in this country," Lord I'Volmer said, " go on and prosper," aid let them have more employment by Tariff Reform. "Let us have a trade union for the British Empire. Let us keep out foreign blackleg ' made goods, and let us be e ble to pay the trade union rate of wages. But we say no man shall be forced to pay for political opinions with which he does not agree. We are sure about our programme; we are sure abou ourplatform ; we are not ashamed of our policy. Wet will put it before the country at the next election, and lam certain that we shall win." (Cheers).

CO-OPERATORS AND THE LABOUR PARTY. The Co-operative United Board, at a meeting in Manchester on Saturday, discussed the question, which has bcen considered at Feveral joint meetings, for a possible federation for certain purposes between the Co-operative Societies and the Labour party. It was decided that "in view of the feeling expressed in the movement on union with other forces, any further action in the matter will be deferred until after the peat Co-operative Congres 8." •

A SHAM FIGHT.

Interesting Manceuvres at Grappenhall.

Red Cross Work.

The grounds of Grappeahall Hall, the residerce of Mr. and Mrs. Aylmer Frost, are just now looking at their best, but, on Saturday we saw a most unusual, yet interesting spectacle there.—The Cheshire (22) Voluntary Aid Detachment of the Brilish Red Cross Society gave a practical demonstration of how the nurses will act on the battlefield. The cadets looked exceedingly smart in their white uniforms, vvith the symbol of the Red Cross on their sleeves, and carried out the demonstration in a highly creditable manner. A large ambulance tent had been erected in the park, and the village schools had been arranged as a hospital station to receive the "wounded "members of the Church Lads Brigade, who engaged in a sham fight in the grounds. About 150 members of the Warrington Church Lads' Brigade took part in the manteuvres. A general idea of the operations was that Lancashire and Cheshire were at war. Lancashire took the initiative and were supposed to have made a successful invasion of Cheshire from Warrington. In the vicinity of Northwich active operations were going on. Lancashire had an advance depot field, hospital and large stereo at Grappenhall. The Red, under Captain Henshall, defended their stores. A raiding party, under Captain Ford, attempted to reach the hospital and stores to set fire to them. About 4 o'clock Captain Henshall received news from Captain Edwards that he was attacked, small bands of the enemy having been observed in the distance. Signalling was carried on from the church t3wer, the two canal bridges, and the hospital. Eventually the two forces mot and a keen fight ensued, the casualties numbering 21, nine cases being treated , in the hospital and 12 cases in the field hospital. The range of experience and knowledge required for the work is well illustrated by the following list of cases treated : —Wound of varicose vein of right leg, compound fracture of shelf of humerus with 1 artery injured, bullet wound through left lung, bullet wound of left thigh with artery injured, bayonet wound of axillary artery, enteric fever (isolation hospital), shell wound of lower end of right thigh, bayonet wound over pit of stomach, bullet wound with fracture of tight sixth rib and injury to lung, crushed fingers, fractured jaw, scalded leg, foreign body in eye, sunstroke, sprained right ankle, scalp wound, bullet wound of left thigh, fracturing femur, severe bleeding from nose, fracture of left collar bone, bullet wound passing through left knee joint., faint. The " patients " who went to the hospital were given first-aid treatment in the field by the C.L.B ambulance corps, under Sergeant-Major Bottomley and Instructor Gibson. The Chief Constable had kindly lent the wheel ambulance for the boys' use. There were also four stretchers, one having been improvised with rifles and coats. Major Brooks, Captain Sankey and Major Appleton acted as umpires. The most interesting feature of the afternoon was the demon- stration by the nurses. In the school hospital Nurse Callender was in charge of six cadets, the others being on the field and in the dressing station. They carried out their dutit s in a praiseworthy manner, proving themselves to be fully prepared for any emergency. After the demonstration, the nurses and the work they had done were inspected by Major Motphew, R.A.M.C., of the Mi itary Hospital, Bury, who after- wards addressed the nurses. He expressed his entire satisfaction with everything he had seen. He thought the nurses carried out their work in a most excellent manner, and be also said the schools were most suitable for the purpose. Subsequently, the Boys' Brigade and the nurses were entertained to tea by Mr. and Mrs. Frost, to whom a hearty vote of thanks was accorded at the close. The officials of the Red Cross Society (Cheshire 22nd) are: Commandant, Miss M. Broadbent; Lady Superintendent, Nurse Callender ; Quartermaster, Mrs. A. Frost. The occasion of the demonstation was the second annual inspection, and amongst those present were the above officials ; Colonel Mocatta. hon. county secretary ; Major Sidebottom, county director ; with the following township leaders : Mrs. G. Pekin, Mrs. E.GDavies, Mrs. Gordon Roberts, Miss Ethel Appleton and Miss S. Broadbent (vice. ,president Daresbury division). Others present were : Mrs. Lyon, Mrs. and Miss Broadbent, Mrs. Ussher, Mrs. Evans, Mr. and Mrs. Farrant, Mrs. Arthur Willis, Major Green, Captain and Mrs. Elgood, Mr. Alymer Frost, Mrs. Gibbons Frost, Mr, and Mrs. Lloyd and Mr. and Mrs. Ainsworth.

The Examiner, Saturday, June 21, 1913. MILL TO PULPIT.

BLESSINGS OF THE INSURANCE ACT.

The Rev. W. Cooper, who will shortly leave Warrington for Leigh, recalled the days when he toiled in a Lancashire factory, in the course of a speech at an open-air meeting held by the Progressive Labour League, at Bridge Foot, on Monday evening. The rev. gentleman's early experiences have given him a real and abiding sympathy with the working- classes in their struggles and trials, and, although reared in a Tory atmosphere, he was led to throw in his lot with the Liberal party, because he believed that only from them could ameliorative measures be expected.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0067.txt | included=true | included =====
REARED IN A TORY HOME.

Mr. COOPER, who presided over a large gathering, said there was cause for congratulation in the Lent that their opponents had not yet recovered from the attack of blue funk from which they had been suffer- ing for a long time. Although they might try various remedies to pull them round, he was afraid that their case might be regarded as hopeless. Concerning the Tery party, there was a great deal that one might Eay , which would not do them the most credit. For a long period they had matters pretty well their own way, up to a very memorable election, but they had not done much for the workingman. If they had, he (the speaker) would not have been on that platform. He was reared in a Tory home and used to swallow all that his father told him, but when he came to weigh up the record of the Conservative party for him- self, he found it was hardly the party for a workingman. (Hear, hear.) The day of democracy was coming rapidly, and the Conservatives knew it as well as anybody. That was why they pretended to intro- duce a certain measure dealing with housing reform. The question which occurred to him was : Why did the Conservatives never try such a plan before, when they had matters in their own control, with a full working majority in the House of Commons, and a House of Lords which would say "Ditto "to all they passed? They never troubled about housing then, but now, in order to try to curry favour with the workingman, they were prepared to introduce some impossible legislation. It was too late in the day to try that game now. (Hear, hear.) The party now in power had so won the affection, esteem and confidence of the public that they were prepared to trust them for a long time yet with their political destinies. (Applause.) He trusted that, even after the next general election, the Government would still have a fine working majority. (Applause.) TORIES' DESPICABLE GAME.

The Conservative party had played a mean and despicable game during recent months. The National Insurance Act would win its own place in history as one of the finest measures the world had ever seen. (Applause.) The Government had taken the best from every nation and built and improved upon it. As far as possible, the Conservative party purchased the Press and did their bebt to ruin one of the most humane measures ever conceived. Even yet, they were so successful in their dust-throwing that a lot of people could not realise that the Insurance Act was something who would do them good. (Applause.)

EXPERIENCES OF FACTORY LIFE. "In Warrington," continued Mr. Cooper, "you are not a typical Lancashire town, and the conditions here are not paralleled by those in any Lancashire town I know. Under the factory system, especially, there is scarcely any provision made for sickness or for any who may be in need. I have toiled among thousands of those people, both as a weaver and as a minister of the Gospel, and I find that, it is one of the most brutal systems going. As things are moving to-day, a man cannot make the home he would like to on his wage, and becaule his wife can earn money there is a great temptation for her to go to work too. That means that in many cases the house is neglected, the family is dragged up instead of being reared, and scarcely any provision is made for sickness. In the mill where I was employed there sore about 2,000 workers, and, with the exception of 150, they were all on piece work which meant that as soon as they ceased working their wages stopped. What, did that mean in sickness ? Twenty-five years ago it meant that we would pass round the hat and make a collection in order that they might be tided over the diflicllty, and the firm would second those efforts by doubling the amount collected. But through competition and increased expenditure on living, that went beyond the reach of b )th the firm and ourselves and it had to be stopped. You had then well.nigh 2,000 people in one factory, and if sickness came there was to be no free doctor, or medicine, no grant of so much a week, no help beyond charity for any of their needs. You in Warrington have other arrangements than that. At your wireworks and at several of your leading firms, you have sick funds. That is a very exceptional thing. WARRINGTON A THRIFTY TOWN.

" Having bean in Warrington three years, I realise that you are a most thrifty set, in comparison with what obtains in most parts of Lancashire. We are never really upset here by bad trade, ani because of the good money that many of you earn you can make pro- vision, in conjunction with your fellow-men, for times of adversity. Therefore you hardly retlise the blessing the Insgranca Act is to the taeming thousands in Lanci- r hire." (Applause.) TORY SLANDERERS BROUGHr TO BOJIC.

In conclusion,Mr. Cooper said he hoped those present would not forget the shameful way in which the Tory party had used the Press against soma of the finest men in the Kingdom. He was glad they had been brought to book about that, but even now some of them wauted to keep up the lie that the Liberal party was a dis- honoured party. Warrington Progressives, he was con- fident, were in full sympathy with the Liberal pro- gramme, which was by no means played out, but waa helping forward the day of true democracy. (Applauae.)

===== 0002601_19130621_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
RETRIEVING A REGRETTABLE MISTAKE,

Mr. ERNEsT CLARKE, of Manchester, delivered an address. He said that Warrington held a high place in Lancashire Liberalism. A good, organised cimpaigu was always steadily carried on here, and they had DO fear but that, when the time came, Warrington would be able to retrieve that regrettable mistake which gave Mr. Harold Smith a position of notoriety. There were only two alternatives before the working people in the politics of to-day—action and reaction. Our country had been built up by action ; Warrington had been made what it was by the action of public-spirited citizens. A policy of reaction—which was the correct term for Toryism—could surely not appeal to workingmen in these days. The whole gamut of Liberal policy was in line with the practice of caution and moderation, tempered with justice and what was absolutely necessary for the working people. It was not sufficient for Liberals in these days to speak merely about Home Rule,Welsh Disestablishment and electoral reform—great and necessary as those measures were. They had to direct attention to questions of social reform, of bread and butter, work and wars. He defied any man to point to a single ameliorative measure that came before U 3 in the political life of to-day, and which would do anything to uplift the standard of the °Ammon people, which had not the hearty support of the Liberal party. (Applause.) It was not on a barren record of promises, but on eight years of solid performances that they could appeal to the country. Old age pensions, the reform of many abuses in connection with Parliament were some of their achievements, and he claimed that the Liberal party had been in no small degree responsible for the creation of a public opinion which insisted that a statesman should pursue his way very cautiously in the matter of directorships, contracts and shares, and that never again should the country return to that state in which a Tory Chancellor of the Exchequer sold his estate at a lucrative price to the Government of the time, and director after director sat on the Tory benches and were intereatel in contracts.

LIBERAL MINISTERS VINDICATED, We could never return to the old Tory methods by which, as the Empire t•xpanded, Chamberlain contracted. (Laughter and appliu3e.) The Liberal ministers who had recently been attacked, had been proved absolutely innocent of any charge against their political honour and integrity, and the men who assailed them were skulking behind rumours of "It might have been," and " They should not have done it." (Laughter.) The sole interest of the Tory critics was not the welfare of the country, but the desire to induce the people to return to power those who were responsible for the mess and muddle which the Liberal Government had to clHar bp an 11)06, To speak of Tory democracy was like referring to a summer winter on a white black. (Laughter and applause.) Having referred to several measures of the Government in detail, the speaker said Liberalism was the bulwark that stood between the people and the power of the trust which sought to dominate their lives with regard to their food supplies. Toryism stood in the way of trade unions obtaining the security and freedom which they ought to enjoy. Liberalism opposed Toryism in its attempt to fetter the people with the reactionary system of conscription and all that it implied. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19130621_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CARSON CIRCUS.

Mr. E. WYNNE JONES said there would not much longer be need to discuss the Home Rule question. The Carson circus bad gone on tour—(laughter)--but it would soon return, empty-handed, Nothing that Sir Etisia‘d Carson and his followers might do could injure the coming into operation of that great measure of self- government for the Irish people. (Applause.) Mr Hamilton, the successful candidate in the Altrincbam election, had a great deal to say about the people of this country being tired of legislation that was carried at the dictation of the Irish party—implying, of course. that the Liberals simply bad to carry out the dictates of John Redmond. If they were going to remove the Irish members from the House of Commons, they must not only remove the Nationalists but the Irish Tories too. They would then find that the Liberals would have
