===== 0002601_19130621_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
THE NEW FREEDOM.

President Wilson's Tariff Policy: Its Bearing on Free Trade and Protection.

WAGES IN AMERICA.

[TO THE EDITOR OF THE "EXAM INER."]

Sir,—l confess there are several statements in President Wilson's book which have surprised me very much, and had I seen them in any newspaper or unauthorised publication I should not have believed them. That has been the chief difficulty in discuss- ing the Tariff question. The so-called facts and etatisfcs have been in many cases contradictory, inconsistent and misleading. But there is no feaaon whatever for doubting Dr. Wilson either on the score of ability or of integrity, so I think his facts may be accepted by all parties, however we may differ about his opinior s. The first statement which calls for notice is on page 200 : A Poser. Do you know that about 90 per cent. of those who are employed in labour in this country (America) are not employed in the protected industries, and that their wages are almost without exception, higher than the wages of those who are employed in the protected industries? " This seems a poser for British Tariff Reformers who have been contending that Protection generally or necessarily increases wages. But is there something unexplained ? Are there any circumstances connected with the Federal p:4icy of restricting foreign imports which makes the so-called upprotected industries of America only free as regards home competition and not free in the sense that British industries are free? If that is not the explanation we must ask insistently why are wages so much higher in America? President Wilson says:— " It is susceptible of convincing proof that the high- paid labour of America—where it is high-paid—is cheaper than the low-paid labour of the continent of Europe." What is the convincing proof to which he alludes? Presumably he means that they work harder, that there is more skill and enterprise,a bettor selection of the fittest, less sympathy for the unfit, and in consequence a relatively lower cost of production. President Wilson is not fighting for Free,Trade, as some people imagine. The new freedom which the Democrats want is a free tariff, free from special privileges and favouritism to free the country from high and unreasonable tariffs. That President Wilson is not aiming at Free Trade will he quite clear from the following passages, p, 145 :—" But ! ' exclaim those who do not understand what is going on, you will ruin the country with your Free Trade ? ' Who said Free Trade? Who proposed Free Trade? You can't have Free Trade in the United States, because the government of the United States is of necessity with our present division of the field of taxation, between the Federal and State Governments supported iu large part by the duties collected at the ports " ; p. 150: —" Let me repeat there cannot be Free Trade in the United States as long as the established fiscal systtm of the Federal Government is maintained." " The Federal Government has chosen throughout, all the generations that have preceded us to maintain itself chiefly on indirect instead of direct taxation. I daresay we shall never see a timo when it can alter that policy in any substantial degree; and there is no Democrat of thoughtfulness that I have met who con- templates a programme of Free Trade." Protection and Employment.

As to whether Protection does or does not generally incresse employment, President Wilson does not say anything very definite. In the case of America the gigantic trusts and combinations have interfered so much with the natural law of supply and demand, cf labour, as well as of material, that it is almost impossible to judge what the state of affairs would be without such interference. President Wilson tells us that "as soon as these combinations see prices falling they reduce the hours of labour, they reduce production, they reduce wages, they throw men out of employment," and after arguing that the garden of protection which they have been cultivating must now be weeded, that favouritism and other obnoxious growths must be rooted out, he as : " he policy of freeing the country from the restric- tive tariff will so variegate and multiply the under- takings in the country that there will be a wider market aid a greater competition for labour." It will be seen from the passages I have quoted from President Wilson's book that he says nothing against Protection as a principle, but he wants to free it from privilege and favouritism, and from every kind of pi isate arrangement. It is to be hoped he will succeed, but it is a Herculean task. I have more than once expressed an opinion that Great Britain cannot be guided, to any large extent, by the experience of America or asiy oilier country respecting tariffs, because the facts and conditions vary so much and President Wilson's investigations confirm that opinion. The geographical position, the supply of food-stuffs and raw materials and our mercantile supremacy make it a difficult problem to solve. It would be solvcd much sooner if it wee discussed by all parties with a little more candour and consistency. Our Board of Trade returns prove nothing of any consequence, and periods of prosperity neither ; prove that we are right or that Protectionist countries are wrong, since all prosper at the same time, There is only a difference of degree between the state of ' things in this countiy and that which President Wilson condemns so much in America. Combinations, private arrangements, and secret understandings are here also regarded as signs of commercial cleverness. But we are more hypocritical about it and call it Free Trade.

No Voting Without Knowledge. On the other hand many of our Tariff Reform manufacturers whilst condemning free imports don't want to lose any advantage which they now obtain by getting cheap raw material. So is it likely they will vote for any plan which may injure their own industry or which raises the cost of production ? Will work- ingmen vote for a plan without knowing how their employment may ho affected? Commonsense dictates a negative answer to these questions. Hence an Authorised plan should be published now for examination and discussion. If it is economically sound it will outlive every kind of ridicule or criticism. I am aware, of course, that such straightforward action would be quite a new departure in politics, but I believe it would be appre- ciated by the people who, as a rule, dislike secrecy in public matters. The people have a constitutional right to know every detail, and politicians should in every case be told that secrecy will be looked upon as weakness or insincerity. This is the keynote of President Wilson's book, and my concluding advice to the electorate is, away with secret government and secret policy of every description. Freemen need no guardians and no leading strings. Publicity is the only thing which can keep politics and public business in a pure and healthy state. Even our law courts were adopting secret methods until a recent decision compelled them to open doors again. More light means more freedom, more justice, more equality, and more humanity. Let this be the war cry of all Reformers 'light, more light," and they will find that progress will be more rapid than they ever dreamt of. "Keep it dark " is the policy of all who thrive on public plunder and despise the people. In the latter half of the 19th century there were signs that the dark ages had passed, but I have doubts about it now. How to begin is the practical question. Let every candidate for Parlia- ment or public office be asked: "Do you believe in Light or Darkness ? " That would be a step forward. W. CHRIMES. P S,—l 5m rather sorry my first article has not lleen criticised or commented upon, as a little discussion on such important questions is very desirable.

BISHOP OF LIVERPOOL AND FIVE-MINUTE SERMONS.

Preaching in Southport on Sunday, the Bishop of Liverpool stated reasons for church-going, and, speaking of sermons, said there were some who thought it was quite enough if they had five-minute or ten-minute sermons. He was asked the other day to preach for five minutes at a certain place, and he declined to do it on the ground that it was dishonour- ing the ministry of the Word. Qn the other hand, there were p?ople who thought of nothing but the sermon. When he wa.;, a curate people used to say to him when he urged them to come to church, " I will come and hear you sometime." His reply was, "Do you suppose that I have come round to ask you to hear me preach? I should, indeed, be conceited to do such a thing." There was a. common expression which was heard, "to sit under Mr So-and-So," as if the one object in coming to church was to sit under a preacher and listen to what he had to say. The sermon was a most important part of a service, but the end of preaching was praying, and if the sermon did not make them pray the sermon did not fulfil its object,

Rev. W. Cooper Recalls the Days when he was a Factory Worker.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0071.txt | included=true | included =====
DITTON DISASTER.

Injured Passenger Recovers £6OO

Damages.

AN AGONISING EXPERIENCE.

A claim by a passenger who was injured in the rail- way disaster at Ditton Junction last September was heard in the Liverpool Assizes, on Monday, when, before Mr. Justice Lush and a special jury, Samuel Lloyd sued the London and North-Western Railway Company to recover damages. Mr. Gordon Llewart, K.C., stating the case for the plaintiff, said that his client was at one time a gymnasium instructor, and at tie time of the "notorious Ditton railway disaster" was employed as overlocker in a hismit works and also as caretaker of a Wesleyan Mission Hall, earning altogether the equivalent of £2 Bs. per week. On September 17th last the plaintiff was a passenger to Liverpool in the train which came to grief. He was travelling in the front compartment of the second c ;Itch, and when the train got to Ditton Junction, said counsel, "partly rs the engine was' antiquated, partly as the driver was admittedly incom- petent, and partly as the train was going at sixty instead of fifteen miles an hour, the engine was derailed and the carriages wrecked; and, if any further ingredient of horror were needtd, a few minutes after the disaster had happened the wreckage took fire." FROM DAYLIGHT TO DARKNESS.

The plaintiff was thrown across to the opposite side of the compartment ; he wraii further thrown about the compartment, and the next thing he realised was that from broad daylight it had become completely dark. He was involved in wreckage, and was unable to move anything but his left foot and the fingers of his right hand. He lay there for a considerable time shouting in vain, and in the meantime observed a smell of gas and discovered that the wreckage below him was on fire. The fire reachPd him, and it was only after the right side of his face and neck end a portion of his bead round about the right ear had actually been burnt that the rescue party arrived and extricated him with difficulty. He was first placed on the station platform, and then put into the room where the fifteen persons whose death had been involved by the disaster were lying. He asked to be removed, and was taker_ to the hospital, where his injuries were attended to. He was afterwards taken home, where he lay in bed for nine weeks with his right leg in splint 9. He bad not yet recovered, and how long the effect upon his nervous system of that horrible agonising experi- ence and of his actual injuries might endure the doctors would state that no one could tell. In fact, there was great doubt as to whether he would ever be able to resume either branch of his employment, while the caretaking job he had lost for ever. The plaintiff gave evidence bearing out counsel's opening statement. For the defendants, Mr. Sanderson, KO., called evidence to prove that the injuries sustained by the plaintiff were not so serious as he had sought to estab- lish, and that, the anxiety caused by the action having been removed, his recovery would be accelerated, towards which end resumption of work in two or three months would materially contribute. The jury awarde I the plaintiff £6OO damages, and his Lordship gave judgment accordingly.

A REPAIRING LEASE.

If you have a house on a repairing lease, naturally you take care of it. You do not want to spend more rroney than necessary at the end of your term. Quite right; that is only common business prfidenee. But what about the most important repairing lease of all. Your body is held on repairing lease. Perhaps this has not occurred to you before,but every time you take food it is nothing more than building up or repairing the body. Admitting the absolute and indisputable necessity cf body-building, it is advisable to use only the best materials. Which are the best materials? They are those substances provided by Nature to sustain the edifice. Nature is no jerry-builder. She uses small but good material, like the little Currant, that wonderful product which the art of Man can never imitate. That best of all nutrients, grape-sugar, con- stitutes the greater part of the Currant. Grape-sugar is pure and powerful nutriment, Its effects are last- ing. That is why people who eat plenty of Currants enjoy the delights of robust health. They are keeping their structure in perfect repair by using the finest substance provided by Nature for the purpose.

NONCONFORMIST TEACHERS.

Not Excluded From Church Schools In Warrington.

At the meeting of the Warrington Education Com- mittee on Monday evening, Mr. J. Jarman asked the chairman of the School Management Committee (Rev. Canon Stevenson) whether it was the fact that in Warrington positions in the non-provided schools were practically close] to Nonconformists, and, if so, whether that fact was taken into account, other things being equal, in tilling positions in the Council schools. Canon STEVENSON : The posts of assistants in non- provided schools are not closed to Nonconformists. I have frequently appointed Nonconformists in our own schools.

Mr. JARMAN : It is the case in some towns. Canon STEVENSON : That may be so, but there is no reason why it should be so. The CHAIRMAN (Councillor H. Roberts) : As far as I know, I don't think there has ever been any suspicion of anything of that kind in connection with our schools in Warrington. Mr. JARMAN said there appeared to be a general impression that p)sitions in Church schools were closed to Nonconformists, Ho thought it was a mis- understanding that perhaps wanted clearing up, and that was the ieason why he asked the question. Councillor WILSON said that at Christ Church Schools, Latchford, before they were taken over by the Council, they had more Nonconformist female teachers than Church teachers.

RETURN OF THE TERRA NOVA.

The Terra Nova, the ship of Captain Scott's expedi- tion, arrived at Cardiff on Saturday, and the members of the expedition were given a warm welcome by huge crowds. Lady Scott and her little son joined the vessel at Penarth, and spoilt some time on board.

Their opponents had been making much of the by-elections, but he challenged any Tory to show that any Government in the history of this nation had lost so few by-electicns as had been lost by the Liberal party of recent years. (Applause.) THE COMING LAND CAMPAIGN.

Referring to the future work of the Liberal Govern- ment,Mr. Jones said they were going to deal drastically with the land system, which lay at the root of the industrial problem. The Tories talked about tariffs,but we had a tar worse tariff at home than any which the foreigner could raise against us. We allowed landlords to withhold from use the land which was God's great gift to the human race. The result of that system was that we had the idle rich at one end of society and the workless poor at the other. When the Govern- ment took up that question, the Tories would forget Marconis in their hatred against Mr. Lloyd George for the facts that would be revealed in that agitation. Ile (the speaker) believed the Government would adopt such a sygtem as would prevent countrymen flocking i,lto the towns and depressing the rates of wages. If they could be guaranteed a decent living wage many men, who were now swelling the labour market, would be only too willing to go back to the land. (Applause.) The Government were also determined to ensure that the people who created huge land values should have a just share of the increment towards the relief of their burdens. (Applause.) In this connection, the speaker instanced land at Latchford which, owing to communal activities, had appreciated in value in the course of 100 years from £3 to about £6OO per acre. Mr. .1. CorrEfuLL also spoke, and said no Government had done so much for the benefit of the workers, and of the country as a whole, as this Government had been able to accomplish. (Applame.)

THE SOLWAY PRINCE.

Apportioning the Damage Caused bi the Collision in the Ship Canal.

• In the Admiralty Court on Thursday week, s Justice B trgrave Deane decided the case of the owner of the Solway Prince, Liverpool, v. the owners a the Rossmore, of Glasgow. The collit.ion took place in the Manchester Shit/ Canal at 12.30 p.m. on 22nd March, 1913, betwee° Thelwall Ferry and Statham Wharf. The Sol/la Prince was on a voyage down from Partington vii!uee cargo, when her steering gear jammed. Her evgln_ were put full speed astern and her whistle WI three short blasts to the Rossmore. The Plaietitio alleged that the Rossmore c une on at consideru speed, and instead of keeping properly on the son% ward side of the canal caused the collision, resulted in the Solway Prince foundering On"' immediately. ter Ihe defendants denied negligence, and oil e, claimed for damages, alleging that the Solway Pr'e?, not being under proper control, came upon the 'Al' Bide of the canal and 93 caused the coll,sion. tbe His Lordship, having heard the evidence, in -1.0 course of his judgment, said that the initial cause of collision was the breaking down of the steering of the Solway Prince. The master of that vele; knew at some time that the apparatus was defent'fb; and upon subsequent examination it was found ted r wrong. Upon consultation with the Brethren he was not altogether sure that the blame rested upon the negligence ifhin resulted in the steering gear being wrong. was obvious that the other vessel was to blatnrci because she broke one of the rules that they Bhe_!l4 not go at a greater speed than six knots. The u°" 0 was of opinion that the Rossmore, with sufficient Or when she saw the plaintiffs' vessel was steering et; lessly across the water, ought to have stopped engines. It was evident that something was and steps should have been taken to have acoideu eh collision. Time was careless navigation on of sides, and the only question was the degree liability. The Court adjudged the plaintiffs to P 94 three-fourths of the damages, and the defendants eel fourth.

Judgment accordingly.

EDUCATION COMMITTEE'S DEBATE. Schools That Will Have to Go.

At the meeting of the Education Committee on Mon- day evening, attention was drawn to the fact that the Medical Inspection Committee had considered the report and resolved that the attention of the Sites and Building Committee be called to that portion of the report relating to the Echool premises, and that they be asked to take such steps as they may deem advisable with a view to the. defects in those premises pointed out in the report being remedied by the managers.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0080.txt | included=true | included =====
MUCH TO BE DONE.

Mr. Holm AN KING DON, in submitting the minutes of the committee, pointed out that the question of improving the health conditions in the school came up annually. They tackled it two years, ago, and a great deal had been done in certain cases. Tney had had,the co-operation f the Sites and Buildings Committee, and he wished to take that opportunity of saying that they recognised that the committee had done what they could. Judging from the present report, however, a great deal remained to be done. As long as they had a school medical officer and be said that certain improvements were necessary, the committee ought to do their level best to carry them out. When the method by which the defects could be remedied came to be investigated, it might be found that there would be some adequate reasons why in certain cases the suggestions could not be carried out, but then they would have a satisfactory answer why necessary improvements could not be made.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0081.txt | included=true | included =====
A DEFINITE STANDARD REQUIRED.

Mr. R. T. FAIRCLOUGH said •the difficulty of the managers was the varying requirements of successive medical officers of health. He was connected with the Parochial School, which, they were told by a former medical officer, was the best ventilated school in War- riugton. Yet Dr. Hibbert now said it was not sufficiently ventilated. A new medical officer was entering upon his duties, and he (the speaker) suggested that he should have some flied standard with regard to ventilation, or it would be impossible to satisfy his requirements.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0082.txt | included=true | included =====
TEETH AND HEALTH.

Alderman BENNETT said it was an enormous advance to have a report of that kind submitted. He could not help feeling that by following up the matter on the lines suggested they would be doing a great deal to promote the health of coming generations. He was afraid they would continue to buffer more or less from the want of proper sanitary arrangements until some of the old schools had had their day and they replaced them with up-to-date ones. It was highly important that the health of the children should be stunied in every respect, even if it involved considerable expendi- ture. He expressed the disappointment which he felt after reading the dental officer's report in connection with the clinic. Dental treatment was one of the most useful things they had ever undertaken. It was! phase of national health which had been very much neglected in the past. The horrible statistics of dental defects among children, not only in Warrington, but elsewhere, gave cause for considerable alarm. He did not propose to enter into a dissertation on toothache, but is was obvious that it was not merely a matter of personal discomfort, but closely affected the general bodily health. Sound teeth were one of the greatest desiderata if we were to rear a really healthy and reliable race. He was dis- tressed to note how little disposed parents were to co-operate with the authorities in bringing about a better state of things. The report mentioned that a charge of sixpence was made for children receiving treatment where the parent's income was above a certain scale. The amount received in six mouths was only 9s. When it was considered that 1,651 cases were found with defective teeth, and that the number of parents who paid for treatment in accordance with scale was apparently only 18, it was either another proof of the lowness of wages and of the importance of endeavouring to secure better social conditions, or it betokened lamentable apathy on the part of the parents.

MUNICIPALITIES AS A SUB-LUNAR PROVIDENCE.

He was in favour of the extension of the activities of municipal authorities in directions that would stagger some of his friends. He believed that municipalities and bodies like Education Committees should act the part of a sub-lunar Providence and largely regulate the future of the race. There ought, however, t 3 be co-operation on the part of the people to secure the best results, just as we had to co-operate with Providence to make the world better with regard to spiritual and divine matters. If the authorities were to spend money on medical and dental inspection, the least they could expect was that every right-minded parent should assist them, sad, where possible, pay his share.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0083.txt | included=true | included =====
THE COUNCIL'S " WISDOM " TEETH.

Mr. JOLLEY said the real remedy was the provision by the local authority of an adequate number of modern and really first-class schools. If the Town Council would take steps promptly to supply that need the problem on that side was settled. Possibly an extension of the work of the dental clinic might faciliate that. They might provide an additional department where a dentist could examine the wisdom teeth of some of the members of the Town Council. (Laughter.)

WILL NEW BUILDINGS SOLVE THE PROBLEM? Mr. UPSON said the primary object of the committee was to see that the children were educated under the most favourable circumstances, with regard to the buildings and equipment. But did the provision of new buildings, such as they were providing, adequately solve the problem? In the medical officer's report they had condemnation of the ventilation of the most recently erected school in the borough, the plans of which were submitted to the medical officer himself. Yet no sooner was the building opened than the ventilation was condemned. At other Council schools repairs of the playground or buildings were already required. He ventured to state that the ventilation in some of the older schools was not any more defective than in some of the most recently erected schools. THE WAGES QUESTION AGAIN. Councillor PEMBERTON pointed out that the authority did not own all the schools, and the Sites and Build- ings Committee could only approach the managers and endeavour to persuade them to carry out necessary recommendations. There was a great deal in what Mr. Upson had said. Therefore the remarks of Mr. Jolley hardly applied, unless he would add a condition that schools which would really satisfy the medical officer should be built. Whether that would be possible he did not know, but medical officers were constantly changing their requirements. He did not know what the wisdom teeth of the Town Council were. (Laughter.) Perhaps some of the co-opted members were deficient in them altogether. But when they talked of children's teeth they were beginning at the wrong end. All the tooth brushes. &c., would not make bad teeth into good ones. Feeding had much to do with the question, and underlying it was the wages question. If the parents did not get proper wages they could not feed the children. They could form what committees they liked, but they were spending thousands of pounds when it was too late. It was a shame that in one of the wealthiest countries in the world there should be so many children growing up defective, not only in teeth, but in many other respects.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0084.txt | included=true | included =====
CRITICISMS SAID TO BE VAGUE.

The Rev. Canon STEVENSON said it would be of great value to managers, who were most anxious to do every- thing that was rignt and proper, if the medical officer's recommendations were made more definite and specific. With regard to Hamilton-street school,the items referred to in the report had been carried out, but he supposed the continued criticism meant that the medical officer was of the opinion that the work might have been done in a better way, or that something further was needed. The managers were really in the dark, the recommendations being so vague that it was impossible, with the best will in the world, to know what was right and best to do.

Mr. LONG LAND said the managers of the St. Peter's school had carried out their architects' recommenda- tions and he did not know what more the managers could do. On more than one occasion they had written to the Education Committee asking whether, if ceitain work were carried out, it would answer requirements, but they could get no satisfactory reply. EXAGGERATED IMPRESSIONS.

The CHAIRMAN (Councillor H. Roberts) said the impressions gained by some members from their read- ing of the report had led to somewhat exaggerated statements of the criticism of Evelyn-street School. The so-called condemnation merely amounted to an

===== 0002601_19130621_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
expression of opinion that the ventilation of the class- rooms leading off the central hall was not entirely satisfactory. That was a long way short of absolute condemnation. Councillor PEMBERTON : But it is the newest school we have and we expect perfection. The CHAIRMAN: The expressions used to-night would give one the impression that the ventila- tion of the school was condemned. If that is condemnation, it is a very mild form of condemna- tion. Continuing, Councillor Roberts said it did seem hard upon them that, after they had gone to the trouble of having their schools carefully designed, with a view to meeting pre-conceived ideas with regard to ventila- tion, &c., and the plans having been approved by all the authorities concerned, they should now be the subject of adverse criticism. He quite admitted that there was room for a little I protest. With regard to the report generally, his I impression was that very great improvements had ben ' made during the past year in remedying the complaints previously announced by the medical officer. All they had to do was to continue the good work, and so far as difficulties could be removed, he felt certain they would be dealt with. There were, however, certain physical cor.ditions which were incapable of improvement, and the schools which contained them, and which were sufficiently bad, would, in time, have to go our. There was nothing else fur it, but they had not reached that yet. -

===== 0002601_19130621_art0086.txt | included=true | included =====
FULL AND FREE CRITICISNI WANTED.

Mr. KINGDON said the report of the medical officer had not yet been fully considered. It would be unfair to expect a medical officer, from a perusal of the plans, to say that the ventilation would be satisfactory or otherwise. If they put the onus on the medical officer too severely, they would cramp his power of criticism. What they wanted him to give was perfectly fair, frank, free criticisms of the state of affairs in the schools, and then the committee could decide whether it was practicable to carry out the recommendations. The standard in these matters was apt to vary. They could only learn by experience, and the new schools ought, of necessity, to be an improvement on the old ones. He thought that considering that was their first year they had a good record for dental work. Talking of parental co-operation, it would make one's heart ache to hear some of the arguments advanced against ailing children not being treated. It made one wonder whether one was in England or somewhere else. The minutes were then approved. REPAIRS TO NEW SCHOOLS.

Mr. R. T. FAIRCLOUGH, in submitting the minutes of the Sites and Buildings Committee, said he was afraid that considerable expenditure would have to be incurred in regard to Beatuont School before it was satisfactory. Mr. UPSON inquired about the nature of repairs ordered to be carried out to the girls' and infants' play- ground at Bolton Council School. Some explanation should be forthcoming of a position of affairs which necessitated two schools so recently built being put into the repairer's hands. He had known playgrounds put down 20 years ago which had not required any repairs. Yet here was a ground only two years old requiring attention, and they were faced with a heavy expenditure in regard to Beamont School. When they considered the heavy outlay on new schools which faced the ratepayers, they ought not to he called upon to spend large sums of money on repairs to buildings so recently erected. Mr. FAIRCLOUGH said, he did not think there was anything serious with regard to the playground at Bolton School. The DIRECTOR explained that one of the seams had lifted in consequence of the heat, and the ground had to be levelled because it was dangerous to the children. Mr. FAIRCLOUGH added that at Bertmont School the water flooded the cellars when the syphon or pumping appliances failed, and ken had to wade through the water to get to the boiler.. Sometimes the coke got wet, and it was doing considerable harm to the boiler if they stoked up with wet fuel. Something would have to be done to prevent the flooding, or it would be necessary to renew the boiler from time to time. Mr. Fairclough added that very shortly the greater part of the building would require re-pointing. ASSISTING THE DEAF AND DUMB.

Mr. PLINSTON drew attention to a minute in the pro- ceedings of the School Attendance Committee, which stated that owing to the difficulty experienced in finding suitable employment for deaf and dumb children, it was decided to request the authorities of deaf and dumb schools to notify the committee some time beforehand of the intended discharge of a child, giving the nature of the employment for which the child would be best fitted. Ile said a good deal of time had been given by the Director, to ycliom thanks were due, in seeking employment for one of the boys. Notice was requested from the authorities of the special institutions to allow them a better opportunity of looking out for suitable situations in the town. It would be agreed that that was a wise step to take in the interests of the unfortunate children.

TAXI-CAB FATALITY.

Warrington Driver Censured by

Coroner.

At an inquest at Altrincham on Monday, on a widow of 70. named Ellen Higson, of West Timperley, who died in the Altrincham Infirmary as the result of injuries received through being knocked down by a taxicab conveying a party from Warrington to Manchester races on May 15, it was stated that the accident occurred while the taxi was passing a stationary tram which the deceased was attempting to enter. Harry Gordon Robden, of Fletcher-street, Warring- ton, who was one of the party in the taxi-cab, said at the time of the accident they were going at the rate of from eleven to twelve miles an hour. When the front of the taxi was level with the back of the tram the old lady suddenly stepped off the footpath, looking neither to the right nor the left, and went towards the tramcar. The result was that the mud-guard of the motor caught her and knocked her down, throwing her towards the footpath. The brakes of the taxi were applied, and the taxi grazed the tramcar. The Coroner : I suppose you were in a hurry to get to the races ?—I was not particular whether I saw the races at all. No definite instructions were given to the driver. Harry Davenport, of Frederick-strzet. Latchford, the driver Of the taxi, said it was impossible to avoid the accident as the lady stepped in front of the taxi. In reply to the Coroner, he said he did not stop because there was a clear road for him. The Coroner : Exactly ; you are always in such e hurry. You fellows forget that we foot pashengers hays a right to cross the roads whether you sound your horns or not. Did you check your speed ?—Yes, before attempting to pass the car. The witness said he was only going 15 miles an hour. If he had passed the tram on the right he would have knocked down two lady c3clists. The jury returned a verdict of" Death from mis- adventure," and asked the Coroner to censure Davenport and give him to understand that when a tram had stopped motorists ought to pull up to give pedestrians a cnance. In complying with this request the Coroner said he wished the law compelled motoriEts to stop where there were compulsory tram stops.

JAPANESE RULES FOR LONG LIFE.

The Japanese conception of the function of a; newspaper is more comprehensive Than our own. One of the most influential of the Japanese news- papers, Jiji Shimpo, set itself some time ago the task of discovering the means whereby a man could assure himself of a life of 100 years. It arrived at the definite conclusion that this result could be obtained by diligently following twelve precepts, which were formulated as follows: The first four are comparatively familiar. "Spend a much time as possible in the open "Go to bed early and rise early." "Sleep at least six hours and at most seven and a-half hours in a dark worn with the windows open." "Be moderate with tea and coffee: avoid smok- ing and alcohol altogether.' The remaining eight are in various degrees un- familiar. "Eat meat only once a day." "Take a bath as hot as you can bear it once every day." "Wear clothes made of very coarse cloth." "Observe one day of rest in every week, on which you must neither read nor write." "Avoid every outburst of passion, and every excess of brainwork." "Marry; widowers and widows marry again immediately." "Avoid rooms that are too hot, and, above all, those that are heated by steam-pipes." "In order ta have a constant supply of those organs which are worn out by age or exhaustion, you must feed upon the corresponding parts of animals." By the scrupulous observation of those twelve rules a man of normal constitution is assured by Jiji Shimpo of a minimum life of 100 years,

RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

A Prosecution and a Lesson.

The fortnightly meeting of the Runcc-.-n Board of Guardians was held at Dutton Workhouse on Tuesday. Mr. C. E. Linaker, jun., presided. THE COUNTY RATES.

The Clerk reported the receipt of the county precept, amounting to £13,488 Bs. sd. He explained that the police rate was increased from 32d. to id. THE COST OF VACCINATION.

The Local Government Board wrote in reply to the Guardians' communication of the 28th with respect to the arrangements proposed for the performance of public vaccination in the Halton District of the Union, , and stated that according to the Board's records, a J part only of the parish of Halton was in this district, , the remainder being in the Runcorn district. The Board also inquired whether the word "surgery " meant the public vaccinator's residence, if so, this term should be employed in the contract. The fee proposed for the second and subsequent operations was higher except for the fourth and subsequent cases within one mile of the public- vaccinator's surgery than appeared to be necessary in cases where mere than one vaccination or re-vaccination was done at'the same time and place. The fee for the first vaccination was intended to be and should ordinarily be sufficient to remunerate the public vaccinator for the time taken by him to reach the place. The fee for the first vaccination should not, in the Board's opinion, exceed 2s. Gd., unless there was some special reason to the contrary, and they suggested a uniform fee of 2s. 6d. for all cases after the first. The Clerk said that the Local Government Board were referring to an old contract when Dr. ' McDougall was the public vaccinator. Since that time the boundaries had been altered, and now the part mentioned was a portion of the Halton district. The officer's surgery was just near his residence. In regard to the contract, the fees were the same as those arranged with the late Dr. Carruthers— that was to pay half the amount paid for the first opera- tion if second and subsequent visits were necessary. This meant that where the distance was a mile or more from the surgery the fee paid would be 3s. 9d. for the second and third visit. The Local Government Board suggested that the fee be a uniform one of 2s. 6c!. The officer said that there were very few cases on the extreme limit of the boundary. The amended fees, as suggested by the Local Govern- ment Board, were unanimously endorsed. A SUCCESSFUL PROSECUTION.

The Clerk submitted a report of the prosecution taken by the Guardians against Ada Nixon, Rusholme, Manchester, who was tried at Iklancheater last week for ill-treating a child named Wilkinson, who had been taken into service from the Workhouse. A fine of £5 and costs had been paid. Since the trial the assistant clerk applied to the defendant for wages earned by the girl during the seven months she was in her service,and £3 was paid on the date fixed for payment by the clerk. Mr. Morris said that showed they looked after the interests of the children placed in their hands. Their best thanks were due to the assistant clerk for the way in which he had got together the facts for the prosecution. The Clerk said the officers of the Board were only too glad to be able to de what they could to meet with the approval of the Board. The Chairman said they were exceedingly grateful to get so far. It was a credit to the officers of the Board It would also be a lesson. Mr. Hallows : To bad mistresses. (Hear, hear.) MATRON'S SALARY INCREASED.

- ------ - Jr. Morris moved that the salary of the matron be increased to £.50 in consideration of the extra duties. Mr. Wright seconded, and the motion was carried.

HEALTH OF THE DISTRICT.

Dr. Adams, medical officer, reported one case of pulmonary tuberculosis in Halton and three cases of other forms of tuberculosis, two in Frodsham and one in Weston Point. There were three cases of diphtheria from Frodsbam. The report was adopted.

COMPULSORY TRAINING IN NEW ZEALAND. New Zealand magistrates are committing large numbers of youths to military detention in forts for refusing to take the oath and do their drill under the compulsory training scheme. The Minister of Defence has been informed by anti- militarists that unless the boys imprisoned on Rips Island'in Lyttleton Harbour are released within forty- eight hours preparations have made for 500 men to take possession of the barracks. The Minister regards the threat as seriously meant, and has promised to inquire into the position.

TOO MANY EXAMINATIONS. LESS EXCITEMENT AND MORE PEACE WANTED.

A conference of head mistresses at Cheltenham on Saturday, passed a resolution asking for the curtail- ment of examinations, urging the necessity for their co-onlination, and pressing upon educational authori- ties the undesirability of children under sixteen being entered for public examinations. Miss Douglas (president) said preplration for public examinationl absorbed too much attention, time, and mental and physical energy of both teacher and pupil, and if schools were to justify their exist- ence as providing for the formation of character and the ripening of children's talents there must be less excitement and mcre peace and quietness.

UNCERTIFICATED TEACHERS. Cheshire Committee Refuses to Endorse_ " Retrograde " Step.

At a meeting of the Cheshire Education Committee at Crewe, on Monday, Dr. Hodgson presiding, a resolu- tion was received from the Winsford and Middlewich District Committee suggesting that "in view of the difficulty in obtaining uncertificated assistant teachers in the county the Education Committee be requested to consider the advisability of the re-adoption of the former system of training pupil teachers, so that the deficiency may be met an more uncertillcated teachers provided." Mr. T. Bamford (representing the Cheshire Teachers' Association) contended that their present method was far superior to the past. The Cheshire Education Com- mittee had made great efforts to improve the type of teacher. To take the step suggested would be most. retrograde. The result would not be to improve the type of teacher ; it would only be supplying the deficiency with an inferior and unqualified class of teacher. He moved that the resolution should not be adopted. The Rev. Grant Bird seconded the motion, and the resolution of the District Committee was disapproved. The Rev. Grant Bird announced that a letter had been received from the Registrar of the Manchester University expressing the gratification of the Council at the action of the Cheshire Education Authority in providing University scholarships in agriculture.

VARICOSE ULCERS ON LIMB.

105, Churchill-r,ad, Norwich, Eng.—" I had seven varicose ulcers, one a very large deep one, almost to the bone, making my leg look completely raw. The ulcer began with just a small red place, but in a few days was larger than a five-shilling piece and then kept spreading with ever so many smaller ones break- ing out around, so as to make the leg look almost raw with inflammation. The discharge was awful, and the pain almost unbearable. My whole leg seemed as if it were on fire. I dreaded the nights coming and could not bear my leg to be in the bed at all; I had to rest it on a chair by the side. I could not sleep at all either night or day while it was bad. I could not get across the room without help, as I could not put my foot to the ground. "It lasted eight weeks in all in that terrible state. I had it treated, but it broke out again. I washed the raw places with the Cuticura Soap and then spread a little Cuticura Ointment on a piece of rag and laid it. over the places. I only used the Cuticura Ointment six weeks, and now there are no signs whatever of any raw places or inflammation. It seems like a miracle to be cured in such a short time." (Signed) Mrs. Stamp, Jan. 30, 1913. Cuticura Soap and Ointment are sold by chemists everywhere. A sample of each with 32-p. book post-free from nearest depot. CHAMBERS OF COMMERCE ON COMPULSORY

TRAINING.

Responding to a reqnest from the Prime Minister, the Association of Chambers of Commerce has forwarded a memorandum embodying their views on the provision of " such a course of universal training in discipline and arms for the youth of the nation as will meet the urgent necessity for adequate national defence." The Association represent that the question of national defence should be non-party ; that aerial navigation has rendered necessary a reconsideration of the whole subject ; that the defence of the country is left to a small body of volunteers ; and that some employers refuse facilities for employes willing to undertake training. The Association consider that some system of compulsory training is essential, and that all employers should be compelled to afford.. facilities.

The Examiner, Saturday, June 21, 1913.

The Passing Week.

THE ROYAL VISIT.

Another fortnight and we shall be on the eve of the Royal visit to our ancient town. The arrangements are now entering upon the final and most strenuous stage and there is every indication that Warrington will rise to the occasion and make their Majesties' first day in Lancashire not the least memorable of those they will spend in the County Palatine. Kindly forethought has , dictated special provision for the comfort of two sections of the community which are well entitled to consideration on such an occasion. The little band of army veterans who have served former Sovereigns on the field of battle are to be allotted positions close to the Royal Pavilion at the Town Hall, where they will be able to see the ceremonial to the best advantage. Thetfootpath on one side of Sankey-street, between the West gate of the Town Hall and Crosfield-street, is to be set apart for cripples, who otherwise would, amid the pressure of the huge crowds, find it impossible to witnes the Royal procession.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0100.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ANTI-SMOKE CRUSADE.

The fact that there are two sides to every question could not be better exemplified than in the special interviews, which we published last week, with reference to the new Smoke Abatement Bill. His Worship the Mayor, who does not regard smoke as a very deadly enemy, sees in the Bill an unwarrantable attempt to over-ride local authorities, and to compel the adoption of means of consuming smoke in advance of scientific knowledge on the subject. On the other hand, Alderman A. Bennett, ever in the forefront of the anti-smoke campaign, welcomes the measure and hopes it may speedily become law. He thinks it likely that local authorities will be stimulated to more vigorous action by the knowledge that the Local Government Board may, if it con- siders them too lethargic, take the smoke question out of their control and appoint special authorities and inspectors to deal with it. Meanwhile, it ,is encouraging to know that W arrington can now boast a second smokeless works, in the shape of the large industrial establishment of the Richmond Gas Stove and Meter Co., Ltd., on the banks of the Manchester Ship Canal, at Grappenhall.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0101.txt | included=true | included =====
TO ASSIST CONSUMPTIVES.

The Phthisical Care Committee for War- rington,the formation of which we announced last week, signalized its first actual appear- ance this week by changing its name. " Phthisis," which literally means a wasting is little understood. Consumption, though too well understood by long usage to describe a terrible scourge, is popularly used in referring to the disease as it attacks the lungs. But there are other forms of the disease besidesthat of the lungs. Tuberculosis has the advantage of being increasingly well understood ; it is also comprehensive, for it includes all phases of the disease in its raid upon the skin, bones or organs, any of which may be attacked by the tubercle bacillus and it expresses a complete idea that neither of the other words does. The Committee, there- fore, named the mselves the Tuberculosis Care Committee. As would be seen from the list we publisled a week ago, the committee, which is thoroughly representative, has been formed to look after the general interests of consumptives, whose working efficiency and ability to command situations and means of acquiring suitable food supplies are sadly affected by a long-drawn out failure in health. Nothing but the best of wishes and the sincerest form of co-operation can be in store for this committee, the promoters of which are to be congratulated upon the sub- stantial advance they have made in the work of preliminary organisation.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0102.txt | included=true | included =====
OUR HOLIDAYS.

It is difficult to understand, until one inquires somewhat deeply, why we matter- of-fact English people make such a dreadful mess of our annual ,holiday. Why, may we ask, do _people insist on invading the pleasure resorts in almost uncontrollable droves in one month of the year and leave the same resorts more or less desolate for the remain- ing months? The arrangements mean that there is great waste in both directions. The wealth that the seaside or the hills have to offer in the matter of health-giving qualities runs absolutely to waste for the greater part of the year, when it could be commanded at a cheap rate, while we overcrowd to suffoca- , ton point in August; and, although we suffer all the inconveniences and dis- comforts that flow from the crowding and jostling that take place, we have to pay the most ridiculous price for our accommodation. It puzzles one to see how any sane person can consent to sleep in a bathroom at night, and be neglected in the , crush by day, at a popular sea side place in August, when he might have had a whole house to himself and be waited upon like a lord at half the price if he had only made I his presence felt in June. Three or four I decades ago the matter was of little moment, I because the holiday habit had not spread to the upper working classes. That, perhaps accounts for some of the mischief. What was considered the best part of the year was originally selected, and the additions to the ranks of the holiday makers have been grafted on to the old system, until we see the modern period of concentrated activity that for a month or a little over upsets the equilibrium of our railway companies, drives railway men to despair, and makes lodging- house keepers into a species of honest and justifiable blackmailers. The poor holiday maker very often asks himself if he is really enjoying himself,and the fear is that, in his heart of hearts, although he goes with the crowd, he concludes that he would be much better off in comfort, health and pocket at home. There are many contributing causes to this unhappy concentration, and some of them cannot very well be got over. There are the school holidays and the family ties, for instance. But there are people who have no family ties or children to consider, and they might, with advantage to them- selves and others, take their pleasures at other times. But they wish for the company of those who have family ties and so the aggregation is kept up. Many object to taking their holidays early in the year; they prefer to have a rest late to prepare them for the fatigues of the coming winter. There is, however, every reason why those who can should keep clear of August, not only in their own interests, but the interests of those who are forced to go from home in that month.

AINSDALE.

Wide low land, and far grey sea, Lonely, shell-sown strand. And chanting winter winds, and free. Aged dunes and peaty land, Sea-birds piping mournfully, And everywhere the flying sand. J.C.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MARCONI DEBATE.

Liberals will regret, as Mr. LLOYD GEORGE and SIR RUFUS ' ISAACS regret, that these gentlemen should have done something that subsequent events have shown to be indis- creet ; but they will be indignant. at the malignant and poisonous campaign in which the Opposition, hungry for office, persist with a bitterness and hatred, such as, at all events, honest men can hardly understand. The committee appointed to inquire into the Marconi tran- sactions of these two brilliant men, unani- mously acquitted them cf all suspicion of corruption. The men indicted so unscrupu- lously before the nation frankly admitted their fault up to the point where fault lay. But what of the Opposition? Baulked of the intended victim over which, vulture-like, they have been circling for so many weary months, they remained vicious and avaricious to the end. They brought forward a vote of censure, and although Mr. BALFOUR showed both sides a way out, his friends refused his lead and persisted, until out-voted in their venomous demand for a verdict which the evidence had never warranted. They over-acted their part. If their cry for purity in public life had been as sincere as their effort after party gain was clear, we could have borne with their unctuous rectitude. But they were merely intent upon destroying the man who, single- handed, has done more for the people than the whole host of them can ever hope to do. They hate him and his work with a fierceness that will stop at nothing. Fortunately for the nation and its toiling millions they have missed fire, though there are signs that they will be unable readily to forsake their unsavoury methods or discard their forlorn hope. The habit of maligning opponents, when such an incentive is the directing force, is not easily lost.

UNFAVOURABLE GROUND FOR TARIFF REFORM.

The Trade Returns issued during last week have a double interest. They go to show that the CHANCELLOR OF THE EXCHEQUER is pro- bably on safe ground in budgeting for increased taxation without increased revenue. And they add one more touch to the ruin of the Tariff Reform case that our industries are "gone or going" under Free Trade, and throw further back into a dim and distant future the prospect of those two black winters on which the Tariff Reformers build their only hope. During the past month imports rose by £6,211,000 and exports of British goods by £5,026,000. It is in manu- factured goods that the rise in exports is most marked. The total increase over May, 1912, is nearly six millions; iron and steel account for nearly two millions, and cotton goods for over a million. Mr. CHAMBERLAIN, and his successors in the Tariff Reform propaganda, have been singularly unfortunate in choosing such unfavourable ground.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
Warrington_ Art Student's Success.

We are pleased to announce that Sydney W. Clatworthy, a student at the Warrington School of Art, has gained the senior art scholarship, value £6O 'a year for three yerrs, awarded annually by the Lancashire County Council. Mr. Clatworthy has previously achieved several notable successes. This latest success reflects great credit not only upon Mr. Clatworthy but upon the School of Art which, under the headmastership of Mr. Clarence Mawson, is worthily maintaining its highest traditions.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
Illness of Mr. Frank Turner.

While attending a fire demonstration at a fortnight ago, Mr. Frank Turner, jun., deputy super- intendent of the Fire Brigade,caught a chill. Pneumonia and congestion of the lungs developed, 'and his condi- tion for a time was serious. We are pleased to state that he is now on the high road to recovery.

Athletic Successes.- Several members of the Warrington Athletic Club achieved successes at the Wigan police sports, held at Springfield Park on Saturday. W. Siddall was first in the 220 yards flat race, his start being IGI yards and his time 23 seconds. A. Siddall took the honours in the half-mile flat, doing the distance in one minute 59 seconds, from a 38 yards start. T. Siddall was second in the 440 yards (37 yards start). Choir Trip. The annual trip in connection with the choir of the Church of the Sacred Heart was held on Saturday, when New Brighton was visited. On arriving at Liverpool, the party, who were accompanied by Miss M. Warren, organist and Mr. J. Rossitter, conductor, inspected the various places of interest, including the Lady Clippel of the New Cathedral, after which they crossed by boat to New Brighton, where tea was partaken of. A ramble on the sands followed. The arrangements were carried out by the choir librarian, Mr. G. Coggins. The Property _Market.

- - Mr. Frank Hickman offered for sale by auction at the Blue Bell Inn on Wednesday, the dwelling-house, 38, Arpley-street. Bidding commenced at A:200 and, alter keen competition, the house was sold for £365. Mr. T. Ridgway was the solicitor to the vendors. At the Old Music Hall on Thursday, Mr. Hickman sold by instrections of Mrs. F. R. Roberts, York Drive, Grappenball, a number of household appointments and a collection of works by eminent artists. Paintings by Oswald Garside realised 81 and 91 guineas and some by Woodlock from 3 to .1." guineas. A small picture by Macdonald was puicbased for three guineas. Other pictures realised fairly satisfactory prices. _

===== 0002601_19130621_art0108.txt | included=true | included =====
Tuberculosis Care Committee.

The first meeting of this committee was held on Tuesday at the Town Ball, under the chairmanship of Mr. F. Aylmer Frost. There was an excellent attend- ance. The committee decided to adopt the name of tuberculosis in connection with its title, and not to confine its efforts to pulmonary cases alone. Various aspects of the problems before the committee were briefly discussed, and it was decided to appoint two sub-committees which should report in detail upon present conditions before any action is taken. One sub-committee will concern itself with the provision of work for sufferers from consumption. The other is to report upon the provision of nourishment, clothing and separate beds. Dr. G. W. N. Joseph and Mr. C. E. Parker were appointed delegates to the forthcoming conference on consumption to be held at Westminster. Mr. Wilfrid Rowland was appointed hon. secretary to the committee.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
Marriage of Miss Markel.

The marriage, announced in another colunan, of Miss Markel, is a social event that will interest many Warrington people. The wedding! was, we under- stand, a very unobtrusive affair, the only celebration being a quiet family lunch at the Savoy. The bride is, of course, a native of the borough, her father's association with Warrington dating back now more than a quarter of a century. In this relation it will be of interest to some of the younger generation, especially in view of recent developments, to recall that Dr. Markel first made the acquaintance of Bank Quay as a representative of Messrs Brunner, Mond and Co., for whom he came over —a young man almost fres'i from University and Polytechnic—to introduce there certain processes. The task he performed so successfully that on its completion he was induced to remain. Since then he has been completely identified with developments at Bank Quay, both on the soap and the chemical side, of the huge works, of which Warrington is so justly proud. Mr. Murray Morrison is well known in engineering circles as the popular general manager of the British Aluminium Company. Woods' Tutorial College : 94 Successes in

What Their Majesties will see at Alessrs. Crosfields' Works.

'llell7,ll' Majesties will next be conducted to ,ery gnie room, where quite a number of old 4)re ants will be at work. They include the • zPan,llammersley,whose record of service y• Years ; John Willett, 46 years • Randles Ltiatiecacrs ; T Welsby, 27 years; and Samuel works:4s?n, 11 years. In this section of the the soap is run in a liquid state

through shoots into frames, in which it is allowed to set. After it has hardened, the sides of the frames are taken off, and the soap is ready for stripping and cutting. This is done by means of a wire, fixed in a suitably arranged gauge which cuts the soap into slabs of the requisite thickness. The old employes engaged in the latter process will include : Peter Johnson (foreman), with 40 years' service, Peter Gleave, 34 years' service, and Alex Losh, 21 years' service.

, . The Oldest Employe. It is in the soapery that George Weave, who has the proud distinction of being the oldest employe of the firm, will await their Majesties' arrival. He has been with Messrs. Crosfield for the long period of 63 years. and it is interesting to note that he has sons and grandchildren whose services with the firm aggregate 180 years—a splendid record! James Lawton runs Mr. Gleave close with 53 years' service, and James Wood (foreman) has been with the firm 25 years. In the soapery the King will see the soap actually ' being made from the fats and oils which are received from the raw materials department. They are blended in a certain manner to produce the best effect and heated with steam. His Majesty will have his attention directed to two large pans, one containing soap just made and the other with the soap pumped out. Each pan has a water capacity of 140 tons.

Their Majesties will next enter the experimental soap laboratory, where the various manufactures of the firm, other than soap, will be show in bulk. They will include silicate of soda, cement, caustic soda, &c. Passing into the toilet room, their Majesties will see the manufacture of soap, chiefly for export purposes. Albert Rowlinson, the foreman, has been with the firm for 22 years. Making Paper Bags. The party will next proceed to the paper bag room, where small paper bags are automatically made from plain sheets at the rate of thirty or forty a minute. These bags are used principally for packing carbosil, the well-known washing soda. The foreman of this section, William Tynan, has seen 13 years' service. In the sample room, through which their Majesties will next walk, one of the oldest workmen will be James Roughley, who has served the firm for 39 years. The tour of the works will conclude in the milling room, where the girls will again greet their Majesties with song. Although they will be in the works for only three- quarters of an hour, their Majesties will see all the processes connected with soap making, with the exception of the melting out of the fats.

A Holiday with Full Pay. The day will be observed as a holiday, on full pay, and all those workpeople who are not actually required to work during the visit of their Majesties will be assembled in the wcrks yard. It is here that the most enthusiastic scenes will probably be witnessed. Their Majesties will, on emerging from the milling department, pass along a raised platform down the middle of the yard, on their way to the time office gate,by which they will depart, and they will take their leave amid such a volume of cheer- ing as will doubtless make their first day in Lancashire a pleasantly memorable one. The works Territorial Company and the fire brigade will parade outside the works, and the Territorials will furnish an escort. The band will be in posi- tion in the yard at 1.30 p.m., and will play selections until 3 p.m. when it will take up a position between the general office entrance and the works entrance, in readiness to play the National Anthem on the arrival of their Majesties. After the party has entered the offices, the band will return to its position in the yard, and discourse music while their Majesties are in the works. Special places will be set apart for pensioners, reservists, and retired volun- teers. Accommodation will be found for the wives of men with over five years' service.

The inside pf the works will not be decor- ated, but the front of the offices will be embellished in an elaborate style fully worthy of the great occasion.

Guarding the Works. In order to guard against all possible emergencies, Messrs. Crosfield are taking special precautions to guard their works, and policemen ,in plain clothes are engaged as watchmen.

ANDREWS' PICTURE THEATRE.

At Andrews' Grand Electric Theatre, during the whole of next week will be shown the great dramatic picture "In the clutches cf the Apaches." It is a very fine subject, and deals with the adventures of a hunch-backed boy, who, after a long and arduous search, saves the little daughter of a French officer from the clutches of the ruffians who have captured her. The incidents take place amongst some beautiful scenic surrounding in Paris and Nice. The film is interesting from start to finish, and takes nearly an hour and a quarter to show. The remainder of the programme will be changed as usual, on Thursday.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
The Examiner, Saturday, June 21, 1913.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
CRIPPLES' JOY DAY.

Motor Ride Through Beautiful Cheshire Country. ---- VISIT TO FRODSHAM.

Thursday was a real joy day. for over 120 of the cripples of Warrington. For the time being their afflictions were forgotten, as swiftly running motor cars bore them through the leafy lanes of Cheshire and gave them the opportunity of breathing pure air and seeing fresh scenes in one of England's most favoured pastoral districts. The cripples motor ride has now taken its place as a firmly established feature of the local calendar. Thursday's was the fourth outing of the kind and its gratifying success was evidence not only of the appreciation of the cripples, hut of the fact that local motorists a, e at.' generously disposed as ever to co-operate in brightening what must, in many cases, be drab and monotonous lives. The Cripples Gui d, which has its headquarters at Bold-street Wesleyan Church, is of course, primarily responsible for the promotion of the trip which is onlyl one side of its beneficent work. During the winter months the Guild is engaged in a systematic effort to show the cripp:es that someone really cares for them and desires to alleviate their irksome lot. Classes ore held at which the boys are taught basket making and drawing and the girls sewing and fancy needlework. Then in the summer comes the crowning joy of the motor trip to some picturesque rural resort. Although the Guild owed its inception to denominational fore- thought, it is quite unsectarian in its actual operations. Need, apart from any question of creed, is the only test.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0121.txt | included=true | included =====
JOY AND PATHOS.

Happy and animated as was the scene in Bold- street, where the cars mustered at noon on Thursday, it had also a poignant blending of the pathetic. Lio helpless were many of the clippies that they had to be carried into the cars, but there were many willing hands to render this kindly service. Others, with the aid of crutches, displayed a surprising degree of nimbleness in taking their places, but to the onlooker it was saddening to note that all bore some signs of physical deformity. However, the prospect of the outing caused their faces to be suffused with the brightest of smiles—sufficient compensation to the workers who had striven so hard in their interests.

One of the most active of the many helpers was Mr. W. H. BeMan who, as marshall, was here, there and everywhere, supervising the arrangement and load. lug ot the cars. Thanks to his tactful guidance, every- thing was accomplished without the slightest bitch. It took nearly an hour before all the cripples had been got safely aboard and then the cars passed in procession along the main streets, making quite a brave show. Once on the country highroads, the procession quickly extended itself and the dust nuisance was therefore practically nil.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
A DELIGHTFUL RIDE.

A sympathetic Providence must watch over the cripples'outing,for never has it been marred by unfavour- able weather. There were many anxious hearts when, after a long spell of brilliant conditions, Thursday dawned with lowering skies, a chill wina and every prospect of rain. At noon,however,there was a striking change and when the journey began,the sun was shining genially from a blue sky. A breakdown threatened later but, hippily, only a few very slight showers were experienced and the children were thus able to enjoy the country side—now looking at its best in its livery of green—to the fullest extent. Frodsham was the nestination, but it was reached by the circuitous Delarnere Forest route which embraces some of the most charming and richly wooded scenery in the county. Gardens were ablaze with flowers and Borne of the roadways were arched by fine, spreading trees.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
AT THE FRODSHAM ORPHANAGE.

The outward journey ended at the Frodsbam institu- tion of the National Children's Homes and Orphanages, which owe their inception to the late Dr. Stephenson. Standing high on the hillside the home, which is a model of its kind, commands an extensive view of lovely country, and receives its full share of bracing air. The cripples were not slow to make friends with the dwellers in this attractive_ spot, and soon everybody seemed much at home. It was, indeed, a novelty to see a number of the afflicted boys engaging in a game of football with great zest and keenness. An excellent tea was provided in the fine memorial hall end, the guests' wants having been attended to, the chauffeurs and helpers were entertained. The whole of the pro- visions had been brought from Warrington in a van placed at the disposal of the Guild by the Alliance Box Company. Alter tea the thanks of the Guild to the motorists, and particularly to Mr. Bellian, were voiced by the Rev. H. Walliamson and Mr. W. Twiss. the latter of whom read letters from the Mayor (Councillor Dr. Joseph) and the Director of Education (Mr. J. Moore Murray), regretting their inability to accompany the party, and wishing the outing every success.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0124.txt | included=true | included =====
CRIPPLES TO SEE THE KING.

Mr. BeWan evoked some enthusiasm by announcing that he had received a letter from the Town Clerk announcing that special arrangements were being made in order that the cripples of ‘Varrington might have a good view of the King and Queen when they came to Warrington. Mr. Bellian also took occasion to express the indebtedness of the Guild to Miss Gornall, who, he said, had given it invaluable assistance at a time when it waa in financial straits.

A short concert was then given, to the great delight of the cripples, a well varied programme being provided by the children of the Homes, assisted by Mrs. Arthur Booth, and Miss Carrie Hastings. At seven o'clock a start was mde for home, and 'Warrington was reached in three-quarters of an hour, after a memorable outing.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====

District Teachers' Association conceived the idea, some time ago of organising and conducting a children's athletic festival, and their efforts have been highly successful from the point of view of encouraging the prowess of the children, who join whole-heartedly in the sports. This year's event had been fixed for the 9th inst., but, owing to the inclemency of tbe weather, the function was postponed until Monday, when the sun shone gloriously, and everythipg was conducive to outdoor games. On the ground at Wilderspool, where one is accustomed to seeing mud bespattered burly Rugbymen, were hundreds

PUBLIC AMUSEMENTS.

ROYAL COURT THEATRE.

"A Fool in Paradise."

The unorthodox nature of A Fool in Paradise " at once appeals to all who love a really stirring drama, and it is not surprising that such an excellent pro- duction has been cordially appreciated at the Royal Court Theatre this week. It is a thrilling story of love, hatred and revenge. The Earl of Norton falls in ' love with the captivating Natalie Winter, who avenges some wrong done to her family by the Norton's by professing love for the Earl, and eventually leading him up the altar steps, into "A fool's paradise." The Earl unsuspectingly trusts his unscrupulous countess, who, however, indulges in various flirtations. She endeavours to crush the Earl by making his I:fe a veritable inferno. He is driven mad, but eventually he recovers himself and j ins his own home circle. Miss Dora Eastlake and her capital West-end company including the great emotional character actor, Cecil Gray, present the play in a very realistic manner. Mr. Cecil Gray has ample scope for clever acting, as Dorian, and he makes the most of his opportunities. His actin, is powerfully impressive, and it is little wonder that when he "took the curtain" he received round after round ,of applause. Miss Dora Eastlake, as Natalie gives a fine portrayal of the part of the vampire woman, infusing real life into the character, and making it as repulsive as possible. Mr. Reginald Garland is well placed as Captain Jack Granby, and Mr. Howard Brewin is a carital Captain Vivian. Miss Phena Powell acts well as Amelia, the mother of the Earl. The comedy is supplied by Mr. Denham Evershed as Lieutenant Reginald Dean, Mr. Hayman Aberhara as Michael O'Connor' Miss Malka Scott as May Somers and Miss Philis Moore as Lady Diana Granby. The other characters are also well sustained. The sensational drama " The Midnight Mail" will be presented next week, when something out of the ordinary, in the was of stage effects, will be seen. PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

Morny Cash, the popular Lancashire comedian, heads a good bill at the Hippodrome this week, and he is delighting and amusing large houses each night with his rare dialect humour Morny has an inimit- able style on the stage, and knows just how to render a humorous song, making the most of each line. He greatly pleased with his song about " Ninepence," but it is perfectly safe to say that he scored a far greater hit with his rag-time parody. Be is also a clever patterer, and caused roars of laughter as the policeman, who was escorting a "little girl" home. As a matter of fact the "girl" is bigger than the policeman, and the audience are nearly sent into hysterics when the " girl " makes such suggestions as "Carry me, officer." Two of the smartest juvnile entertainers we have seen for some time are the real McKays, who appear in "The soldier's return." The boy proves himself to be a really clever comedian, punning being one of his chief accomplishments. The girl sings. a homely Scottish song in a creditable manner, and ably aids the boy in this highly diverting scena. The Shent One present a new and original vocal and. dancing scena and are well received, As dancers, this trio are in a good class, and they present their "turn" amidst beautiful and effective scenery. They dance exceedingly well in Indian, Polish and Dutch costumes, and at the finish give a good sand and buck dance. Rich Hayes is a most eccentric juggler and manipulates a number of rubber balls in a remarkably skilful manner. His make-up is very effective, although somewhat wierd. The Four Paivers present a musical turn which cannot fail to please. They perform on various instruments in a pleasing manner, and score a success in "Irish Melodies," the scenery also being very good. Renee Grahame, is a dashing male impersonator, and is equally successful as the pre- sumptive middy, and dare-devil "recruit." Vera Caine, the athletic girl, gives a cripital demonstration of ball punching and club swinging and is well received. " I saw him first" is an amusing picture on the cinema. "The world's greatest chorus singer," Florrie Forde, will appear next week in her latest success inciudisg "Hold your hand out, naughty boy," and "Dance your troubles away," Sc. Other turns will be : Dawn, the toff of the cycle; Harry Claff, the white knight; Jackley and Le Sine, comedians; Parks and Price, instrumentalists; Hillier and Haynes, the real Scots in their re(a)el American reel, and The Two Britons, athletes.

"The Eve of Her Wedding. " The Royal Repertory Co. have added to their long list of successes this week, when they presented very realistically the popular drama "The Eve of her Wedding." The play is full of incident and the many interestiug situations are well enacted. Large houses have been the rule and all have expressed their admiration of the theatre now that it has been re-decorated and made one of the cosiest theatres in the district.
