RICHARD JEFFREYS,

Frame Maker, Print Seller, Carver and Gilder. and Picture Frames Re-gilded. 88, BOLD STREET, LIVERPOOL.

Looking Glasses

work of to-day was being looked upon as a great extravagance. Basing his subject upon the anointing of Jesus by Mary of Bethany, who used an expensive box of alabaster ointment, be showed how that deed of love was regarded as a great waste. It was, without doubt, typical of the attitude of the world to-day. After referring to the good work which had been done by women of all ages, and to what they might do if only more was entrusted to them, Mr. Evens went on to give examples of the work which was carried on in the missions in various parts of the country, especially referring to his own mission in Liverpool. It was a great labour of love looked upon as an extravagance. It was a marvel to consider the position taken up • with regard to this work. Did men never consider the great extravagance of the love of God, Who in the sacrifice of His only Son for the salvation of the world, was teaching the greet lesson of love? In all departments of life, especially in the world of nature, was this abundant" so-called" waste of love exhibited , by God. But why was the extravagance of all this love to be condemned when, inculcated in the hearts of men, it resulted in the helping and eventually saving of their I fellows? What was wealth compared with the salvation , of the immortal soul? They were, concluded the lecturer, I engaged in a great work, which was not to be looked upon from the standpoint of a monetary value. Mrs. Gipsy Evens interested the audience with tit-bits of the mission work, and also rendered a very effective solo.

The proceeds of the services amounted to about £2O. "The World's Need."

There was a good attendance at the meeting of the Wycliffe P.S.A. Society on Sunday, presided over by the President (Rev. T. T. James). Mr. Sherlock spoke on"Self- sacrifice, "and gave some very striking illustrations of heroic self-sacrifice. There was nothing worth having without effort, work and sacrifice. The world was in need of self-sacrificing men and women. Miss Halton, of Earlestown, sang" Angels ever bright and fair "and "Sun of my soul" in a most effective manner. She is a young vocalist, who gives promise of a successful future. Mr. H. Osborne read the lesson, end Mr. James led the prayer. The Secretary announced that Mr. P. M. Oliver, of Bowdon, would speak at an open meeting on June 29th. An open-air meeting would be held on July 6th.

St. Peter's Church.

There were large congregations at this church on 'Sunday, when the choir festival was celebrated. The preacher in the morning was the Rev. G. W. Dickinson (vicar of Padgate), who based his sermon on the subject of God's love. The Te Deum was rendered to Prior's setting, and the anthem was "Praise, my soul, the King of Heaven." At the evening service, the Rev. J. H. Jones (vicar of Great Sankey) occupied the pulpit. His text was " 0 come, let us sing unto the Lord," and he dealt with the necessity for music in the Church service. Maunder's service was selected and the anthem was Stainer's "I am Alpha and Omega." The soloist was Master Powell. A musical service was held in the afternoon. A quartette consisting of Messrs. Troman, Sudlow, Warburton and Reddish were respons- ible for the rendering of "Love Divine" (Sullivan) and "In the sweet by-and-by (Protheroe). Miss Elsie Hewitt rendered "Come unto Me" and " When I survey." Mr. J. H. Draper contributed" If with all your hearts" (" Elijah ") and "My hope is in the Everlasting" (" Daughter of Jairus "), while Mr. E. Walker sang" Thou art passing hence" and " Come unto Me." Credit is due to Mr. E. A. Padmore, the organist and choirmaster, for the excellent manner in which the choir acquitted themselves.

HATTON.

FARM FIRE.—A tire broke out at Hatton on Wednes- day night at the farm of Mr. H. Mawdsley, who, on returning from town between 12 and 1 o'clock was surprised to see smoke issuing from the building. He immediately telephoned from the residence of Mr. J. Whitley, Hatton Cottage, for the Warrington and Wilderspool Fire Brigade, who were very prompt in replying, and were soon dashing through the village. Thanks to the prompt action of the firemen, who saved a very extensive block of buildings from ruin, little damage was done. No clue has yet been found as to the origin of the outbreak.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0149.txt | included=true | included =====
STOCKTON HEATH.

HILL CLIFFS BAPTIST CHURCH.—The annual summer sermons at the above church have always been considered a very noteworthy feature in the religious life of the locality, and this reputation was uphold on Sunday, when the Rev. H. Motley, of Mirfleld, very ably conducted the services. Addressing the children in the morning, the rev, gentleman based his remarks on "Blessed are the meek," emphasising the futility of boastfulness and conceit, exhorting his young hearers to endorse the true spirit of meekness and quiet determination in the pursuit of those virtues which adorned the life and character of Jesus Christ. The afternoon attendance was very encouraging, although the heat was oppressive. The preacher pleaded with all present to follow the example of the Patriarch Abraham, who instituted family worship by erecting an altar, and also added to the attractiveness of home life in planting a tree and digging a well, the three emblems of protection and refreshment. The evening service will always be remembered, for the church was crowded to its utmost capacity, the aisles and the school being brought into requisition to accommodate the numerous friends. Mr. Motley took for his sabject, "The angels of the sepulchre." He referred to the angels of service, condescension and light, the chief point being that of service. The preacher maintained that only those whose spirituality was of the highest and noblest order, and whose outlook on life comprehended infinitely more than men ' usually associate with religion, were fit to mingle with the mud and dirt in order to bring fallen humanity to God. The choir rendered the anthem "0 taste and see how gracious the Lord is. " Messrs. H. Brocklehurst and Levi Richardson were the acoom- panists. The collections amounted to £27 Os.

LYMM.

FUSILIERS' CAMP.—Much interest was created in local Territorial circles last week-end by a visit of the Salford Battalion of the Lancashire Fusiliers. Com- posed largely of non-commi4sloned officers, the outing was the occasion of some very interesting night out- post duty. The week-end camp, which had been situated in Mr. Johnson's Tannery House field, was struck on Sunday evening, the men returning to town by the 8.28 train.

HIGH LEGH SPORTS.

On Wednesday, the High Legh sports were' held in the cricket field, situated in the delightful park belonging to Lieutenant-Colonel Legh, whose support of the annual proceedings is demonstrated in a very generous manner. The event is of a more social nature than one would infer from the title, and the attendance of the local gentry co-mingling with the tenants and their families and friends, was a pleasant feature. The festival, we understand, originated about fifty years ago in connection with the old-fashioned " Harvest Home," and it was not until the time of the Diamond Jubilee celebrations that it assumed something like its present form. The sports are the occasion for a general half-day holiday round High Legh, and it was a happy baud of children who assembled at the schools, prior to marching through the private grounds of the hall to the park, where, after a hearty participation in the athletic sports, they did full justice to the tea that had been provided for them. fea over, the races for the seniors were carried through, the obstacle race providing much amusement for the spectators. The prizes were distributed at the conclusion of 'each race by the genial hon. secretary, Mr. H. Tunstall, and his two daughters. When the sports were over the Knutsford and Cross Town Prize Band, who had been in attendance during the afternoon and evening, played selections for dancing, which opportunity the young people quickly availed themselves of. The following is a list of officials, races, and prize-winners :—Chairman, Lieut.-Colonel Legh; hon. secretary, Mr. H. Tunstall ; tea com- mittee, Messrs. A. J. Cook, C. Cook, F. Gleave, W. G. Haughton, J. Platt, J. H. Wright, sen., J. H. Wright, jun., select committee, Messrs. J. Price, H. Wright and N. Pedder ; Judges, W Wardell, H. Gibbon, C. W. W. Surridge, and H. Simpson ; starters, Hon. J. E. Cross, Rev. E. H. Edwards, and H. Wright; handicapper, Messrs. J. Price, Owen Davies and N. Pedder. The above gentlemen, together with Rev. H. A. D. Surridge, and Messrs. C. Liecester Warren, J. Cook, S. M. ,Houghton, J. H. Jones, W. Moss, H Tunstall, H. Worthington and J. Wright, jun., formed the General Committee.

NEW BAPTIST MINISTER.

Rev. E.. L. Lovell's Interesting Career.

As mentioned in our last issue, the Rev. E. E. Lovell, of Whitchurch, has accepted the unanimous call to the pastorate of the Lymm Baptist Church. Since the removal of the Rev. J. Back to Canada, a few years ago, the local Baptists have been without a resident pastor, and the coming of the Rev. E. E. Lovell should mean a great deal for the future prosperity of the church. Mr. Lovell, who is a native of Cranfield, in Bedfordshire, received his education first of all at the village school, then at the Middle Class School near Bedford, which, by the way, was almost within a stone's throw of Elstovv, the birth- place of John Bunyan ; and finally, after about twelve years in business, at the Bristol Baptist College. Mr. Lovell com menced preaching in various chapels in the villages round Weston-super-Mare, and as time went on he felt that he was called to relinquish business for the ministry, with the result that he was intro- duced to the committee of the Bristol Baptist College by the Rev. David Davies, then a pastor in that resort. He has held successfal pastorates at Maesteg, Glamorgan. 1884-1888; West Haddon, Northants, 1888-1899 ; Whitchurch, Salop, 1899, until the present time. The reverend gentleman has

Photograph by Priee, 'Warrington TETE REV. E. L. LOVELL.

been president of the Salop Baptist Association, missionary secretary for the county, and also president of the Whitchurch Free Church Council. Mr. Lovell is the proud father of a clever son, who has won several scholarships, and is at present at Middlesex Hospital as a medical student; and two daughters who are teachers, the elder having taken her Inter. B.A. degree. An enthusiastic gardener and an authority on bee-keeping, the pastor has gladdened many a sick person with gifts of tempting fruit and flowers from his garden, and honey from his hives. He also enjoys an occasional day's fishing.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0157.txt | included=true | included =====
AN APPRECIATION.

Importance of Pastoral Work: " Lymm has Chosen Wisely."

My friend and former pastor, the Rev. E. E. Lovell, of Green End Church, Whitchurch, who has just accepted an invitation to the Baptist Church, Lymm, will he an added strength to our Free Church life. His love of, and labours for unity, and brotherli- ness are household words in the district he is leaving, where his genial, kindly, courteous presence will be sorely missed.

It was my privilege to work with Mr. Lovell in many branches of church work, but it was in connec- tion with the Christian Endeavour Society that I knew him best and learnt to value the many good qualities which have compelled the admiration and respect of all classes and creeds, and made his ministry so fruitful. The vigour of the Christian Endeavour Society may be estimated by the fact that for many years it has provided the Sunday School with all its teachers and the church with many lay preachers, and the secret of this success is to be found in the loyalty, esteem and sincere affection which exists between the young people and their pastor and president. The young people at Lymm will find in their new minister a sympathetic friend, a wise counsellor, and one whose high ideals and consistent life will appeal to the best in them. It has recently been said by a well-known preacher that the most important part of a minister's work was the pastoral part, that the greatest need of the church to-day was the home visiting minister. If that is so, Lymm has chosen wisely. Mr. Lovell is a regular and welcome visitor in the homes of his people, and his presence and sympathy in times of sickness, trouble and bereavement has inspired courage and hope and revived drooping faith in many hearts and homes, and endeared him, not only to his own flock, but to many others too.

Mr. Lovell's sermons are carefully prepared, thoughtful and full of Gospel truths. His week- night discourses, usually of an expositing character, are especially appreciated. Mr. Lovell's removal will leave many gaps to be filled at Whitchurch. His work for Temperance, the Free Library, the Free Church Council, the Christian Endeavour Union (extending 18 miles in one direc- tion), and the Salop Baptist Association, will be greatly missed, and he will have the sincere wishes of all with whom he has worked ita happily for the success of his work in his new sphere, and the prayers of many that his work may be as richly blessed at Lymm as it has been in Whitchurch.

ALFRED PRICE, Warrington.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0158.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SILENT ENGLISH.

We English are not a race of talkers, says Evelyn March PhiHipps in the _V incteenth Cen- tury and After. "We all know people," she con- tinues, "who will golf and travel together the whole day, only exchanging a word or two at short intervals, yet who understand one another perfectly and are the best. companions in the world; and no doubt, if good conversation is one of the best things of life, tiresome tal, irrelevant, persistent, empty talk, for talkini-'s sake, can be one of the most exasperating. T',:e talking bore is not by any means always stupid, but, on the contrary, is often very clever and bursting with information, eager to cast his pearls before us; but, as that delightful person, Bagshott, suggests, a diet of pearls is often ex- tremely boring to •the swine. It was Alphonse Daudet who said that what struck him most in England was the silence in the streets and in the trains and omnibuses. . . We may almost say, indeed, that general conversation is so rare in England that many people have never heard any, and the mere idea of their voices being audible to three or four hearers at once is enough to re. eiuce them to dumbness."

IN THE COURTS

WORKS MANAGER ASSAULTED.

Singular Incident at Sankey Bridges.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0165.txt | included=true | included =====
The Girl and the Car Fare.

At the Borough Police Court on Tuesday, before Mr. C. Jackson Holmes (in the chair) and other magistrates, Harry Clemo, 25. Liverpool-road, Sankey, was charged with assaulting George Watson, 72, Hood-lane,Sankey. There was a summons against George Watson for an I alleged assault on Annie Clerno,the 16-year-old daughter of the first defendant. Mr. A. Browne appeared for Mr. Watson, and Mr. Swallow for Harry and Annie Clemo.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0166.txt | included=true | included =====
JEALOUSY ALLEGED.

Mr. Browne said Mr. Watson was a works manager for Messrs. W. D. Houghton Ltd., Sankey Wire Mills. He had been employed there for sixteen years, and had brought out some valuable patterns, five of which were being worked by the firm. This success had caused a good deal of jealousy in the works. He (Mr. Browne) alleged that defendant's action was instigated by another man who was present when the assault took place and urged him to assault the informant. Distantlant was an engineer at Messrs. Crosfield's works. Ho was very friendly with a mein who worked at Houghton's, and who was terribly jealous of the good position which Mr. Watson had attained in consequence of his own ingenuity. On the 28th May, Mr. Watson was coming home from the Hippodrome with Mr. Aspinall, Mill lane, Sankey, on the 11 o'clock car. Miss Clemo got in at the old Post Office. She sat opposite Mr. Watson and his friend. She sat opposite Mr. Watson and his friend. bhe said " Good evening " to Mr. Watson, who acknowledged her. He paid the fares of his friend and Miss Clemo. As a matter of fact, Mr. Watson often paid the fares of a number of persons who travelled on the same car as himself. Mr. Aspinall got off the car at the Bowling Green. At tbe tram terminus the young lady left the car first, Mr. Watson getting out soon afterwards. A number of people,along with the two parties, walked along together, but Mr. Watson never spoke to Miss Clemo until he left to go home at the corner of Hood Lane.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0167.txt | included=true | included =====
ASSAULTED ON LEAVING THE TRAM.

Three days passed and on the Saturday night Mr. Watson went home by the 11.30 car. When he alighted at the terminus he saw the defendant standing near a crowd in which was Mr. Pemberton, the man from Houghton's. Defendant pushed his daughter in front of Mr. Watson and said, " What did you offer my daughter 2e. 6d. for ?" Mr. Watson said he never offered it her. Defendant said, "Oh, yea, you did." Mr. Watson asked the girl, "What did I give you 2s. 6d. for ?" and she replied, "For nothing." Defendant strick him with his fist, felling Mr. Watson to the ground. A motor car passed and nearly ran over him. Pemberton shouted to Olean), "Punch him," but Clemo said," Oh, no, I will do no more." Mr. Watson said something and went on. On the following Monday defendant saw Mrs. Watson at her house and tpld her he had struck Mr. Watson for paying his daughter's fare—which, he contended he had no right to do—and offering her 2s. 6d. for wrongful purposes. He also said that it was not very long since her husband go. a thrashing near the canal, for going with girls. Pemberton was waiting for him outside the house, and when they got together they laughed over a conversation. The man Pemberton was a perfect nuisance to the informant. It appeared that they had been waiting for Mr. Watson several Saturday nights. When struck, Mr. Watson said, "This is a plot." He (Mr. Browne).put it to the Bench that it was a plot by Mr. Pemberton, using Clemo as his tool, to try and lower the man in his employer's estimation. The man Clemo, although be struck the blow' was not the real culprit ; it was the man Pemberton, but Clemo had taken the onus on himself. They might have charged Pemberton with counselling this conduct, but they had not done so because they were going to prefer another charge against him at the Assizes. Mr. Watvon supported Mr. Browne's statement. He added that Clemo was only the cat's paw of Pemberton

===== 0002601_19130621_art0168.txt | included=true | included =====
PAID HUNDREDS OF FARES.

By Mr. Swallow : He had paid huhdreds of fares in tram cars from motives of generosity. He paid for the girl because she acknowledged him as "Mr. Watson." He did not get hold of the girl's arm or give her 2s. 6d. Mr. Swallow said he did not allege that the money was given for a wrongful purpose. Mr. Watson : I never offered her 2s. 6d. Further questioned, he said he never said to the girl, "Hello. dear ; I hope you did not tell anyone of the 2s. 6d. I offered you on Wednesday night." Mr. Swallow: After that, I submit the father came up to you and said " Yes, me." Mr. Watson : The father mentioned the 2s. 6d. and they struck me down. Mrs. Watson said that she and her sister had to go down town on a number of Saturday nights to bring Mr Watson home, in consequence of Mr. Pemberton's con- duct.

Mr. Swallow: It was for no other purpose you went down? Witness: No. Mr. Swallow: Does your husband drink? Witness: He takes a drop, but never gets too much. Mr. Swallow: lam going to submit that he had had some drink on the 28th May. Witness : I don't think he had, because he caught the 11 o'clock car from Rylands-street after he had been to the Hippodrome. Clemo had told her that he had assaulted her husband because be had offered his daughter 2s. 6d. for a wrongful purpose. By Mr. Swallow : Her husband was never interfered with when she and her sister went to meet him. JUSTIFICATION PLEADED.

Mr. Swallow said he was not going to deny the assault was committed by Mr. Clemo, but he was going to call evidence which he hoped would justify defendant's conduct in the eyes of the Bench. He could show reasonable cause for it. The facts were that on the 28th May the girl, who was employed as a checker at Andrews' Picture Palace, got into the ear and saw Mr. Watson, whom she knew by sight. The conductor told her that her faro was paid, but she did not know who paid it, thinking perhaps her father was upstairs and he had paid. When Aspinall left, the girl stated that the car was practically empty. Mr. Watson came across to her and talked about the road.- She did not speak to him when she got off the car at the terminus, nor did she take the slightest notice of him. Watson got bold of her arm, but she attempted to release herself. Watson said, " Don't be afraid." When near the bridge he got hold of her arm again and offered her 2s. 6d., but she said she did not want his money. She released herself, and when she got home she as very agitated and went to bed. The conductor, who was on the car, would say he saw Mr Watson sit near the girl, and further he would say that

===== 0002601_19130621_art0169.txt | included=true | included =====

"I REMEMBER "

SOME ECHOES FROM GRAPPENHALL.

The death was announced in the last issue of the " Examiner " of Mr. Ralph Burrows, of Grappenhall. By the courtesy of Mr Thomas Shakeshaft we have been furnished with copies of some of Mr. Burrows's poems. These recall Grappenhall incidents and we think it will harmonise with the purpose of our "I Remember—" column if we quote some of these poem-incidents here. Their interest lies in the personal references rather than in the poetry, and old inhabitants will perhaps recognise the pictures which are drawn.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0173.txt | included=true | included =====
Sammy Loring's "Pretty Dinner."

One Monday morning early bold Sammy went away To work upon his master's farm which was some miles away. His wife a basket gave him, and it contained she said, A rare good currant pudding, which Ehe for him had made. It chanced that very morning his fellow workmen found A hedgehog in its nest so snug as they dug up the ground. To play a trick on Sammy there and then they did decide, So they removed his dinner —in its place the hedgehog tied. When dinner time arrived Sam to his basket went in haste To where he left his dinner, which he greatly longed

to taste. He ope'd the basket lid and thrust his hand in without heed, When the sharp quills of the hedgehog did make his fingers bleed. He looked liked one distracted, and danced with rage and pain, And vowed that he would kill his wife when he got home again. He left it in the basket and at night when he returned, Against his wife fires within his bosom burned. He said, "A pretty dinner, jade, thou didst prepare

for me-." - " 'Twas good enough for thee or me or any lord,', quoth she. "If it's so good," he said, " perhaps you'll undertake

to eat Such a dinner." She replied, "I do desire no better

meal." "I'll give .it thee," and Sammy then did laugh with

—all his might, For be thought to be diverted by a strange, unusual sight. He thought to see his viife upon the living hedgehog

~ . feast, But without his host he'd reckoned, he had to say the

least ; For when he the basket opened there his dinner was— the men After dinner placed it there and took the hedgehog out again. He asked his wife's forgiveness and gained it very quick, For he saw that he had wronged her, that his mates had played a trick. Legend of Morris Brook. In a poem, "A legend of Morris Brook," Mr. Burrows endeavoured to give form to a tradition which had been handed to him, and ' which was said to explain the presence,in the neighbourhood, of a very uncanny and troublesome ghost. When the plague raged in London town two centuries ago, And laid so many thousands of its population low, That pestilence did likewise through this fair country spread, Slaying many, and inspiring all those left with fear and dread, Yet a mercenary man, who did reside at Morris

Brook, A base advantage of the universal panic took. It was the heir of Morris Brook who coveted the gold Which was hoarded in the house and owned by his

father old. This vile, unfilial son did falsely say his ailing sire Had caught the dread contagious pestilence so dire— This cruel son removed his sire from his own home

and placed Him in a mined hut which sheltered kine from winter's blast, Where he lay securely fastened in, thus a foul deed

. . was done, Thus the old man was murdered by his hard-hearted

son. But punishment both speedy and well-merited o'er-

took The miserable man, for his foul crime at Morrie

Brook' • For after he had squandered his ill-got wealth away, To the life-destroying pestilence the wretch he fell a prey. When death from clay his spirit freed, The Legend says it was decreed It should a beast-like shape assume, And haunt for aye his former home. On dark and Mild tempestuous nights, His grim unhallowed ghost affrights Lone travellers who pass the haunt Of this unearthly visitant. May all the guilty warning take, Repent, and every sin forsake, Ere their immortal souls are lost. Like this vile man's night-wandering ghost. Walking Grappenhall Boundary. A poem "Walking Grappenhall Boundary" describes an attempt that was made to revive the old custom of walking the boundary. The practice was the old method of impress- ing upon the minds of the youths of the village the true extent of the parish. Those who took part followed the boundary line wherever it went, whether through places easy or difficult, water or land. The" Pedagogue, whose name was like a true born nigger's hair" was Mr. Woolley, the schoolmaster. -

BOTh-ig-u-nii-tii—d-c-ild-,-the rich and poor, one morning did assemble, Their purpose was around the village boundary to ramble. A Pedagogue, whose name was like a true-born nigger's hair, With a dozen of his scholars right early he was there. The acts of this heroic band my verse will now record, For great and mighty feats they did perfoim, upon my word. Both housetops and haystacks the lads they did climb Over, And went through bogs and through the midst of many a thorny cover, Some tried to jump o'er ditches which were both deep and wide, But landed in the middle instead of the other side. If anyone unheeding walked too near a river's brink, To push them headlong in that was rare sport some seemed to think. At Lumbbrook there's a culvert dark through which floods roar'd like thunder, Yet two brave youths undaunted this dread place ventured under. An old lady with a broomstrick came to drive them from her garden, When all the boundary walkers laughed instead of begging pardon. When she could not drive them back, she then declared she would not fail To make them walk ere long within the boundary of a gaol. Brave old Jarvis did go underneath the culvert at Cliff Lane, And a large lot of money by his brave act did gain.

COTTAGE RENTS IN LANCASHIRE. SUGGESTED INCREASE AT BLACKBURN FALLS THROUGH.

A suggestion was recently made by members of the Blackburn Property Owners' Association in favour of increasing the rents of cottage houses to meet the increased cost of repairs and general upkeep. The secretary has obtained particulars from a number of other industrial towns similar in character, and as it is found that property owners in Blackburn do not stand at any disadvantage by comparison no action is to be taken by the society as a whole.

GRAPPENHALL SIXTY YEARS AGO.

A Posthumous Article Prepared for the " Examiner " by the Late Mr. Ralph Burrows.

DESCRIPTION OF AN INTERESTING LOCALITY.

[The article which follows was prepared for the " Examiner " by the late Mr. Ralph Burrows. The first portion of it is copied from "The History and Gazeteer of Cheshire," published in 1860, a copy of which Mr. Burrows possessed. The second portion of the article is made up of personal reminiscecces.]

adapted,for the growth of esculents. Tanning of leather is carried on in the village. According to a table of benefactions, Thomas Johnson, by will, dated 24th July, 1627, left the sum of .El 4 per annum, charged upon an estate at Appleton, to be distributed annually to the poor of Grappenhall and Appleton for ever. The above-named sum has for a long period been considered as a rent charge upon the estate in Appleton, which is of greater annual value, but the Charity Commissioners are of opinion that the £l4 has been paid upon the mistaken supposition that under the words of the will the rent cannot be raised above that amount. Grappenhall at one time possessed a village pond or pinfold, which was situated on the south side of Grappenhall Bridge. But it was seldom, if ever, used. Old but Familiar Names. A glance at the directory for 1860 gives usl the names of the principal residents, amongst which we find : Thomas Atherton, shoemaker. Mary Ann Brauston, schoolmistress. Mrs. Ann Bratt, Thelwall Cottage. William Carter, tailor. Thomas Clare, blacksmith. Jam es Daintith. John Davies, beerhouse and timber dealer. William Darbyshire, nursery seedsman. Edward Greenall, Esq., the old Rectory.

Grappenhall Parish comprises the town- ships of Grappenhall and Latchford, which, together contain 2,257 acres, two roods, 30 perches of land, and in 1851 had 658 houses and 3,250 inhabitants, of whom 1,615 were males and 1,635 females, and its rateable value was £12,190 11s. Bd. Grappenhall or Gropenhale itself is a pleasant village and township, and contains many well-built houses, and has a clean and neat appearance. The township contains 1,525 acres, two roods, 30 perches of land, and in 1851 had 157 houses, 788 inhabitants, of whom 361 were males and 347 females, and its rateable value was £3,915 is. Bd,

Watts, Esq., and Leigh, Esq. THE OLD TOLL GATE, KNUTSFORD-ROAD.

Many readers will be glad to see this admirable picture of the toll gate and toll house, which stood near the" Dog and Dart." The gate was rem ,ved in 1876, but the house is yet at inding. Mrs. Astley, the toll keeper, who was a well-known figure, appears in the picture. As this generation knows nothing of toll gates it may be well to explain that a small charge

was made to every driver of a cart or other conveyanc,- who passed the gates. Charges were made for droves of cattle, and for those who rode on horseback, but not for foot passengers. The picture is in the possession of Mr. Thomas Shakeshaft, of Grappenball.

TIIE MIGRATION OF BIRDS AND ANIMALS,

I wrote a few lines a week or two ago about the wonderful distances covered by migrating birds. lam about to say a little more about migration, including a little information about the movements of animals. Do you know that migrating birds have regular routes by which they travel? In North America the valley of the Mississippi and the valley of the Red River is a recog- nised bird route from the Gulf States to the Arctic and the great lakes of the north. Each spring and autumn this route is traversed by hundreds of thousands of feathered kindred, and if you lie awake in Winnipeg city you can often hear, far off in the heavens, the " honk-honk " of the wild geese travelling overhead. The birds traversed this route ages before the history of Canada began, just as they traversed the Nile valley on the other side of the world.

I As the Migrating birds have regular -routes 1 by which they travel, so have the migrating animals. Each spring and autumn the caribou —the great reindeer of North America— travel across country in herds of hundreds of thousands. In Labrador these routes are not merely imaginary lines traced across the map; they are regular highroads. They are known to the Indians as the caribou high- ways, and so immense are the herds that use them that they are, indeed, highways— deeply trodden into the earth by hundreds and thousands of cloven hoofs. Northwards the caribou go each spring—northwards over the great barren lands of Labrador till they reach a country so lone and desolate that white men seldom or never enter it: and southwards they come when winter begins, back to the sheltered forests, many hundreds of miles from their summer quarters. All birds and animals while migrating have some way of communicating with each other in the darkness. The caribou do not call—that would not be safe in the midst of so many enemies—and if a caribou herd passed you at night time, you would pro- bably hear nothing. Yet if you knew what to listen for you would hear it distinctly enough—the cracking of cloven hoofs, and now and then a soft grunt. Nature has so constructed the hoof of the caribou that every few yards it cracks loudly. No other members of the deer family are thus pro- vided, for none of them, except the caribou, migrate at night-time. And it is this click- ing that keeps the caribou herds together during their journeys northwards and south- wards, just as the twittering keeps the birds together.

THE BIRD THAT SINGS. You dear little birdie, who taught you to sing Among the green branches and blo,sorns of spring? I wish you would tell me, for then, don't you see, I'll mk the same person to try and teach me. I wonder, whenever I hear you, if you Have to sit in a tree for an hour or two, And practise your dear little twitter and trill When it is so dreadfully hard to keep still. When you want to play in the sunshine all day, Does somebody hold up a finger and say As solemnly, Now, little blue bird, stay so And carefully practise your do, re, rai, do? ' Do you have to learn about octaves and thirds, And chords and arpeggios and other hard words? And those terrible scales ? Why, of all that Ido I think them the hardest to practise. Do you? Well, however you do, I am sure of one thing, That I have to practise before I can sing ; And with all I may learn, and the best I can do, I never shall sing, I,ttle birdie, like you.

THE WEEKLY SMILE. WONDERFUL ACCURACY.

Mrs. Lane is a zealous and loyal wife, and intends to avoid exaggeration, but has a strong tendency in that direction. "It's perfectly wonderful," she said to a patient friend, "to see the way my husband counts bank-notes at the bank. I think they are so lucky to have him! He'll take a great pile of ten and twenty pound notes and make his fingers fly just like lightning, and never make a mistake !" "Never ?" asked the friend, who knew Mrs. Lane's weakness, and could not forbear the question. " Well—no—at least," stammered Mrs. Lane, "why, perhaps he might get two - pence or threepence wrong, but never any more."

and is now the property of J. H. Leigh, Esq. The other moiety passed from the co-heiress of Boydell to the family of Reddish, whose heiress in or about the year 1556 married into the Marbury family; this part, having been purchased with the other Marbury estate by the last Earl Rivers, is now possessed by the representatives of J. H. Smith Barry, Esq., of Marbury Hall. The Church, dedicated to St. Wilfrid, was erected in 1539, and is built of stone quarried from Cobb's Quarry, Appleton, and is built in the Gothic style, and consists of nave, chancel, side aisles, with a tower of six bells. The nave and side aisles are divided by Gothic arches, springing from handsome octagonal columns. In 1850, the south aisle was carried out to its present extent, by which means ten additional pews were obtained. In 1851, the transept was added to the north side of the church, at which time the gallery was removed over the transept, by which ten more additional pews were provided. These alterations were made in order to provide free seats for the poor of the parish. The expenses were defrayed by voluntary contributions. The church contains a fine organ erected in 1856. In the chancel is a beautiful stained glass window erected in 1850. In the centre of it is represented the Ascension of our blessed Lord, on the right St. Paul's conversion, and on the correspond- ing side Peter and his call. The window bears the following inscription : _

"In memory of John Greenall, Esq., of Middleton Hall, Lancashire, who gave this window, but died October, 1850, before it was finished. November 24th, 1850." There is also a stained glass window at the west end representing the four Evangelists. The living is a rectory, valued in the King's Book at £6 11s. 10d., and is now £542 in the patronage incum-bancy of Rev. Thomas Greenall. In the church are some memorials of the family of Middlehurst, of Latchford. There was formerly the tomb of Wm. Boydell, jun., with his effigy, cross-legged, in mail and surcoat. The Boydells were patrons of the rectory so early as the reign of Henry 111. The stone figures of the Boydells, in armour, noticed by Randle Holme, no longer exist, and the probability is they are buried beneath the flooring (since discovered when the church was restored). There is a neat memorial to the memory of William Brock, Esq., who died May 10th 1764. In 1859, an addition was made in the churchyard, the land being given by the Rev. Thos. Greenall, by which means 400 additional breadths were obtained. The cost of making the addition was raised by subscription. The tithes are commuted for £454 us. ld. The rectory is a neat residence, erected by the present incumbent in 1856. The old rectory is situated a little east from the church. The National School, an Elizabethan edifice, on the west side of the church, was built by subscriptions, and is kept in repair by the parish. The school is endowed with about five acres of land, let for a yearly sum of £lO. The land was purchased with £3l, the gift of Thomas John-son in 1627, who directed the yearly proceeds to be paid to the school- master. The sum of £l7 55., tile produce of some timber cut down upon the estate, has been invested in the Savings Bank. For the rent and interest of the above sum ten boys are taught reading and writing. There are about 80 boys and 70 girls attending the school. Joseph Woolley and Mary Ann Brauston are the teachers. A large room was erected in 1857 for the purpose of hold- ing public meetings, lectures, &c. Grappenhall Hall is a handsome modern mansion, stuccoed, surrounded with pleasure grounds and shrubberies, and is the residence of Mrs. Greenall. Grappenhall Heys, one mile west of the church, a large stuccoed mansion, pleasantly situated in park-like grounds, is the property and residence of Thomas Parr, Esq. Through the village runs the Bridgewater Canal, which affords the inhabitants every facility for conveying their agricultural produce to the Manchester market. John Mainwaring, shopkeeper. Peter Barber, shopkeeper. Thomas Yarwood, shopkeeper. Reminiscences of Grappenhall. The descendants of many of these old residents still live in the village, and their memories are cherished. The Village Schoolmaster. Many of the youths of that day will have cause to remember the old schoolmaster, Joseph Woolley, a strict disciplinarian, and One who turned out in his day many scholars who were destined to fill responsible and important positions in the commercial world and who owe their success in a large measure to the good grounding they received under his tuition. Some of us remember the tasks he used to set before, us when 200 to 300 lines of Gold- smith had to be learned and recited as we stood around his desk (this desk is still in the school), and as we think of him we are reminded of the lines of the poet "There in his noisy mansion, skilled to rule, The village master taught his little school, A man severe he was, and stern to view, I knew him well and every truant knew, Well had the bodiag tremblers learned to trace The day's disa.;ters in his morning face. Full well they laughed with counterfeited glee At all his jokes, for many a joke had he, Full well the busy whisper circling round. Conveyed the dismal tidings when he frown,d, Yet he was kind, or if severe in aught, The love he bore to learning was in fault. The village all declared how much he knew, 'Twas certain he could write and cypher, too, Lands he could measure, terms and tides presage."

It was his custom to take several of the higher standard youths with him when he surveyed the land to give them practical lessons.

Miss Woodward followed Miss Mayy Brauston as schoolmistress, and was asso- ciated with Mr. Woolley as teacher up to the time of her marraige to Mr. Ashcroft, and she still resides in the village, and is the present postmistress. On the south side of Grappenhall Bridge,on the canal side, used to stand a picturesque Swiss cottage, .the residence of the Naylor family prior to their removal to Cuerdon Hall, at one time known as Oyster Shell Hall, from the fact that a rockery was built in the grounds covered with oyster shells. When Roads Were Barred. Grappenhall once possessed a toll bar, and the house still stands near the Dog and Dart Inn. Road tolls were granted by Edward 111., and turnpikes soon spread all over England, as well as Scotland and Ireland, and about 5,000 persons were employed as collectors (not including their families). A board affixed to the toll-house indicated the varying rates of dues exacted from an ass, a score of sheep, a riding horse, a cart, a one-horse jig, a carriage drawn by one or two horses, &c. The tolls were used for the maintenance of

===== 0002601_19130621_art0180.txt | included=true | included =====
roads and managed through a Turnpike Trust. In most parts of the country, toll-bars were erected every six or eight miles, but between Knutsford and Warrington there were no less than four over a distance of about eleven miles, viz., Mere, Hoo Green, High Leigh, Grappenhall. The toll-bar at Grappenhall formerly stood in the Borough of Warrington, on the site now occupied by "Alma Place," near Black Bear Bridge, but it was taken down about 1850 and removed to Grappenhall, the same material being used in its reconstruction.

The toil bar at Latchford and Grappen - hall was kept by Mrs. Astley up to the time it was abolished in 1876.

Since the passing of the Local Government Act, 1878, Turnpike Trusts have entirely disappeared ; the highways now being managed by the authorities of the parish or district in conjunction with the County Authority.

The toll collectors task was not an easy one at times, for many attempts were made to "run the gauntlet" and escape payment, and many were brought to book for their mis- doings, but in this age of inventions, when men are "running to and fro in the earth " on bicycles, motor cars, motor cycles, &c., cutting up the roads, highways, and byways, they have no chance of escaping the impost

LITERARY GLEANINGS.

HOW A KNIGHTHOOD IS CONFERRED. We shall be reading so much in the near future about the King's visit and (possibly) of knighthoods that our readers may like to know what the ceremony of conferring a knighthood is like. The popular notion is that the gentleman to be knighted is tapped on the right shoulder by the King with the accompaniment of the words "Rise, Sir So-and-So." As a matter of fact it is a much more formal affair.

A gentleman to be knighted by His Majesty is to attend in uniform or levee dress, with all his decorations and medals. On being admitted into the Royal presence he advances opposite to the King and makes the usual reverence by bowing, and then kneels on the right knee. The Sovereign then confers upon him the honour of knighthood by placing the sword on both shoulders. The knight raises his right arm horizontally, and His Majesty places his hand on the wrist of the Knight, who raises it to his lips. The knight then rises and retires from the Royal presence with the like reverence that he made on entering.

THE PATRICIAN.

The July number of " The Patrician," just issued,has a varied fare for its select readers, and from its striking new front cover to the back there is a liberal and tasteful selection of fine illustrations. Its first article—on "The German Emperor: After Twenty- five Years "—bears, in spite of its anonymity, the stamp of being the work of one in his Majesty's entourage, and will no doubt attract considerable attention_ both here and in Germany.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0184.txt | included=true | included =====
WHERE AMERICA "SHINES."

"Those who mock American journalism from the standpoint of somewhat mellower traditions forget a certain paradox which partly redeems it. For, while the journalism of the States permits a pantomimic vulgarity long past any thing English, it also shows a real excitement about the most earnest mentalproblems,of which English papers are innocent, or rather incapable. Thus, when a man named John Boulnois wrote a series of articles on alleged weak points in Darwinian evolution it fluttered no corner of the English papers; but in the American" Sun" articles of valuable intelligence and enthusiasm were presented with headlines apparently written by an illiterate maniac: headlines such as 'Darwin chews dirt: Critic Boulnois says He Jumps the Shocks '—or 'Keen Catastro- phic, says Thinker Boulnois.' "—G. K. Chesterton in " Pall Mall Magazine."

===== 0002601_19130621_art0185.txt | included=true | included =====
A POINT FOR SCAREMONGERS.

Here is a point which might be noted with advantage by the conscriptionists and the scaremongers. Canada, an English colony, is bounded on:the south by the United States. Those borders run together for thousands of miles, with not a fort, nor a gun, nor a sentry to safeguard either from attack. And yet peace prevails. May there not, there- fore, asks the " Millgate Monthly," be some- thing in the argument that armaments are a greater danger to peace than unpreparedness for war? The scaremongers would have us believe otherwise, but here, at least, is one instance where peace and an absence of defensive measures have co-existed for a hundred years.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0186.txt | included=true | included =====
" SUMER IS ICUMEN IN."

On Wednesday afternoon a large number of guests met in the gardens of Reading Abbey to witness the unveiling of a memorial tablet of an unusual kind. Nearly seven hundred years ago a monk of the Abbey wrote the part song "Sumer is icumen in." Of the name of the monk we have no know- ledge, but in the oldest copy which dates from 1240, he is described as being of the Abbey of Reading. This copy is preserved in the British Museum, and is the most remarkable ancient musical composition in existence. The musical notes are the " longa," a square , with a stem, and a " brevis," a diamond shaped note. There are two set of words, one in Latin and the other in thirteenth- century Wessen—" Sumer isticumen in." The great interest of the song lies in the fact that it is the oldest part-song in exist- ence. Nothing like it is known to have arisen anywhere either here or abroad for two centuries and a quarter. Upon this, there- fore, England can base a claim to be the birth- place of the part-song, one of the most pleasant forms of choral composition. At the unveiling ceremony the Choral Society of the University College sang the part song, and the Mayor of Reading pre- sided. The tablet is the gift of Dr. J. B. Hurry, the historian of the Abbey.

THOUGHTS WORTH REMEMBERING. BY W. STEWART ROYSTON. If all who suffer were selfish the pity would be for the nurse. Our ideals are not worth much if we flee from them at the first attack. Those who fearlessly shoulder their burden generally find a loyal helper. A good cause to be won will require from us an abundance of self-denial. Looking down on others is not the way to expect them to look up to ourselves. It is the cheer, unheard of men, which helps to make the fight easier for the brave. Whatever the size of the habitation, it is too small for some to dwell content in. We are not here to do what is easiest and most pleasant, but to make less hard the path for the forsaken.

—" Great Thoughts."

Competition. SUCCESSFUL LOCAL HANDICAP.

Saturday was a great day for local bowlers, and, in addition to a full programme being gone through in the Works and" Peter Peacock" Competitions, 72 games were played in the first round of the handicap organised by the Warrington and District Association. In the second round of the club championship, Bull's Head lost to Fearnbead, but on the home.and home games they gained a victory.

-- The concluding games in the second round of the Works Association's Competition were played, and the third round will be commenced to-day.

-- Eight games were played in connection with the "Peter Peacock" Challenge Cup Competition and some good bowling was witnessed.

The eleventh annual handicap in connection with the "Loyal Prince of Wales" Lodge (Warrington District), Manchester Unity of Oddfellows, was concluded on the Cat and Lion green, Stretton. Mr. B. Ball was the winner of the gold medal, and will also hold the Crosfield Cup—given by Mr. A. H. Crosfield—for 12 months.

Several interesting games were played in the second round of the" Harold Smith" Cup Competition, and the green at the Brooklands Hotel was in fine condition.

The results of the day's play were as follow :

ODDFELLOWS' HANDICAP.

---- Prince of Wales " Lodge (MIL

1.0.0. F.

The eleventh annual handicap was played at the Cat and Lion, Stratton, on Saturday. The entries numbered 53. The games were keenly contested, leaving Bros.. G. Bottomley and B. Hall in the final, which resulted in a victory for the last-named, who will be the winner of the gold medal, and holder (for 12 months) of tke Crosfield silver cup—the gift of Bro. A. H. Crosfielbde There are also about 16 other prizes, which will presented at the lodge to-night. The duties of referee were carried out by Bro. T. Reddish. fall before the same player in the semi-final. D. Isher- wood, the well-known footballer, beat his brother T. Isherwood by 13-6, being by far the better man all the way.

In the semi-final, G. Bottomley, who had pulled up remarkably well in all of his games, looked like losing, but as usual he was " there " at the finish, bnd beat E. Lunt by 13-12.

-- Bottomley and Hall, the finalists, are both wire- drawers, and the game was watched with keen Interest. Hall got away with the block, and won finally by 13-S.

J. Allison (Warrington Cemetery) is drawn against E. Nevin (Griffin) in the second day's play in the Manchester section of the individual merit com- petition under the auspices of the Lancashire County Amateur Association.

We wish the Warrington teams every success in the County Competition.

MESSRS. BRUNNER, MOND AND CO.'S

BELIEF IN BOWLS.

A new bowling green at the rear of the Gladstone Crib, Northwich, the cost of which, incluaing the land, the laying, and the equipment with a pavilion, has been borne by Sir John Brunner, was opened on Satur- day afternoon by Mr. J. F. L. Brunner, M.P. The green is 45 by 28 yards. Mr. Brunner, M.P., played the first game on the new green, encountering and defeating by 11 points to 2 Mr. J. W. Deakin, J.P., the chairman of the club. Mr. Brunner said the company with which he was connected were great believers in bowling greens, and therefore at all the works they had opened bowling greens for the men, and he beli eyed they were very much appre- ciated. (Applause.)

The Examiner, Saturday, June 21, 1913.

YES ! EVERYBODY IS WAITING FOR IT.

MARSHALL & WRIGHT, LTD.,

HAVE BECOME SO FAMOUS FOR THIS

GRE T SALE

that Hundreds wait for it every year. Already they are flooded with enquiries as to when it will commence. To those

===== 0002601_19130621_art0219.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SALE IS PROCEEDING

enquirers the reply is

and if YOU wish to get the bargains YOU MUST ACT NOW

What does it mean to YOU?

IT MEANS

Pi Pair of Trousers for Nothing.

With the enormous turnover MARSHALL & WRIGHT have, they make a vast number of short lengths and oddments, and as they do not stock ready-made garments, they have not a ready means of getting rid of them, so to enhance their already great reputation for value they give away at this Sale an Extra Pair of Trousers to

every purchaser of one of their Famous Suits

THE EXTRA PAIR OF TROUSERS 13 FROM THE SAME QUALITY AS THE SUIT YOU BUY.

Thus---If you buy a 2916 Suit you get a pair of 8111 Trousers for Nothing. If you buy a 351- Suit you get a pair of 10/6 Trousers for Nothing. If you,buy a 3916 Suit you get a pair of 1216 Trousers for Nothing. If you buy a 451- Suit you get a pair of 1416 Trousers for Nothing. ANGLING NOTES.

Warrington Workingmen's Angling Association (N.A. Centre).

Last week-end being the last for the close season specials, wore than the usual number of local rods turned out to compete for them, and the pike special was captured on the very last day by W. Sanson, with a pike weighing 4lbs. 9oz. 4drs. Several members had jack to weigh, but when they heard of W. Sauson's capture, they failed to put in an appearance at the club house.

The challenge cup and medals, kindly presented by our patrons for season 1913, will be on view at Kellett's Bridge-street, during next week. Up to the time of sending in these notes the waters round this disttict' are doing very fairly.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0221.txt | included=true | included =====
THE GOWY.

A fair crowd visited here on Saturday and Sunday and several took pike up to 41;lbs. A good catch or two of eels was reported, but trout was neglible as regarded numbers. Keen competition is anticipated here during the open season, as a£l special is offered from headquarters for dace to be caught in club water

NORTON. The canal here was not in good form on Monday, but; Tuesday saw a great change. About two hundred roach were caught by three members of the Gladstone Arms, but the biggest portion had to be returned to the water. Each, however, had a very nice catch, and four eels were also bagged. This shows that up to the present this canal is quite satisfactory. If it should turn out as rumoured, it would be a sad affair for Warrington anglers.

HARTFORD. The Weaver was visited by a few locals, and another eel of about lib. weight was taken. It was a pity this was not creeled on the Monday, as a 15s. special is offered for eels. Several perch were taken and care- fully returned to the water. Warrington anglers are to be complimented this year on the way they have observed the close season. HEADQUARTER SPECIALS.

It should be noted that all members must be in benefit before they can compete for the exceptionally attractive list of specials offered this season. UNFOUNDED RUMOUR.

A report received publication last week to the effect that the Northern Anglers' headquarters were about to be changed to the Bay Horse. No such thing has, how- ever, been mentioned to any of the officials of the Gladstone Arms Club. What was said at a meeting of the committee at the Bay Horse was, that at any time the Northern Anglers of Warrington required a room in which to hold delegate meetings or any other business of that description, the new clubroom at the Bay House would be at their disposal. A.S.

MARSHALL & WRIGHT, LTD.,

THE CYCLIST'S NOTE BOOK.

Some Vigorous Young Cyclists of Sixty and Seventy.

GLORIOUS SONG OF THE WOODS.

[BY "SILVER-STREET."]

If you will refer to the copy of the "Daily News" for 31st May you will see what is possible for not only middle-aged men that I spoke of last week, but for those who are already past the "allotted span," or nearing it. Here are three instances. "Eleven days ago a Scarborough reader wrote: I herewith enclose a cycle tour which a party of five are going to commence in about twelve days' time, and I should be glad if you would send a few names of suitable hotels at the places named. We are all young men ; our ages are 73, 71, 64, 64, and 52 years respectfully.' Then follows an outline of a 14 days' journey to North Wales and back. _ _ _ _

Again: "Just four weeks ago I received a letter from a reader of Christchurch, Hants, who said : I am contemplating a bicycle ride from here to Land's End. Will you kindly give me your opinion as to the best route to take? lam 72 years of age, but quite fit, and can reckon on about 50 or 60 sailes a day.'" One more says: I am 50 years old, and had practically given up cycling, but last summer I was induced to accompany a friend on local tours. Then I got into my stride and in seven weeks covered over 1,500 miles, and I never felt better in my life. I had dinner with one cyclist who had been to London and back from Lancashire. I forget in what time, but be may 68 years old. At Lancaster I met another man who was taking a cycling tour through the lake dis- trict. He was 71 years." I myself am a good deal nearer 50 than I care to contemplate some times, but what with Saturday spins and holiday tours and daily rides to business I cover 4,500 miles a year. Yes, you will answer, but see how tit you are. You are one of the fortunate ones, with abounding health and strength. Am 1? When I was 20, a ride in the train from Wakefield to King's Cross in wet clothes threw me into bed in the big city for weeks with pericarditis. After the closest shave I got out again after 13 weeks' illness, and for years I could not walk upstairs without a palpitating heart, and a series of ugly stabs in that region. I first strode a solid safety in 1889, and with taking it gently at first and disregarding the doctor's orders that I must "never ride up a hill," I have not known where my heart is fox nearly twenty years. So that regarding my fitness on the one band, and my cycle rides on the other, can anyone tell me which is the cause, and which the effect? WESLEYAN CYCLING CLUB.

Twenty-seven members assembled at the Arpley Cannons for the ride to Gateacre. Against such a wind everyone jibbed, and expressed agreement with the captain's suggestion to ride to ClicHord. But it was not to be. Punctures delayed the group and the ride had to be shortened. So Mobberley was first attacked, and then Mrs. Pollitt's provisions, and in the evening, after the eleven miles circuit of Tattoo Park had been completed, the return was taken by Lymm and Thelwall. How many cyclists know what the chorus of the English woods is like, as you come, say, down Cherry- lane to Lymm ? And is it not something worth think- ing about that in every one of the thirty millions of acres in England that same glorious song is going up?

===== 0002601_19130621_art0225.txt | included=true | included =====
CHESHIRE REST HOUSES.

Hardmii's Restaurant, Hatchmere.—Exactly twelve miles from Stockton Heath, swing bridge, lies the chsrming lake of Hatchmere, the nearest point of Delamere Ferest. Hardman's is close at hand, and so are some of the finest bits of the great wood. Besides providing accommodation for large parties of cyclists, covered sheds for machines as well, large pleasure grounds are attached to the house, and lately, I believe a bowling green has been laid out, so that an after- noon and evening can easily be passed there. For the botanist great areas near the lake are covered with the beautiful bogtnyrtle, and he can find the buck-bean, milk-wort of several colours, louse-wort and many species of moss. A good route is through Frodsham and Five Crosses and return through Norley and Acton Bridge.

===== 0002601_19130621_art0226.txt | included=true | included =====
ASSISTANCE FOR THE HEN.

X well-known player, having purchased a country place on Long Island to which there was attached quite a garden, decided to go in one summer for chicken-raising. He bought a cock and half a dozen hens, but could get ro eggs. His chickens seemed vigorous and healthy, but his eggs were procured from the nearest grocery. The player decided to remedy this situation. He replied to an advertisement in a mail-order journal announcing that, for the sum of two dollars, there would be forwarded an infallible recipe for making hens lay. In due time the amateur received this return for his money: To make a *ten lay—Tie a stout string around the hen's body, lay the bird on her side on a board, and fasten the string underneath. If thought desirable, a pillow way be placed under the hen's head.".

The RECEPTION COMMITTEE request the owners and occupiers of property along the route to decorate their premises and join in tendering a hearty welcome to Their Majesties the King and Queen. The Committee also request employers of labour to observe the day as a General Holiday. Streamers or banners over or across the streets most be kept sufficiently high to avoid accidents or interfer- ence %kith the Royal Carriages. Accommodation will be provided for the Crippled Children of the Borough to see Their Majesties. The arrangements are in the hands of Mr. W. H. Bellian, but all applications for places must be made to the Director of Education. GEO. WM. JOSEPH, Mayor.

.nanity is often adequa, R c tabs, .111111°:', 110111-' • amt. -11 1111. II 11, , I • mow "There! I knew you would ask where those little pink cakes came from. I'm so glad you like them. I made them myself with Bird's Blanc-mange Powder. Oh yes! with my recipe the daintiest afternoon tea cakes can be made with Bird's Blanc-mange Powder. These are called Duchess Buns." 2 Eggs. A few Glace or Crystalised Cherries; 2 ozs. Butter. 1 saltspoonful Salt, 3 ozs. Flour, 2 tablespoonfuls Milk, 3 ozs. Sugar. 1 teaspoonful Bird's Baking Powder. and 1 packet of BIRD'S BLANC-MANGE Powder (Raspberry Flavoul-). Put the butter and sugar in a basin and set on the rande to melt. Next beat to a - cream; add the eggs one at a time, beating up well after each, then the milk. Mix thoroughly together the flour, Bird's Baking Powder. Bird's Blanc-Mange Powder, and the cherries previously cut into small pieces: add this dry mixture bY degrees to the liquid ingredients, beating the whole to a perfectly smooth batter. Have ready sufficient small Bun tins which have been well buttered; half fill each of these with the batter, placing a piece of cherry on the top, and bake immediatelY about 10 minutes in a hot quick oven. Be sure it is Bird's Blanc-mange Powder if you wish the cakes to be a success. There are two packets in the 4d. box and four in the Vsl. BmlOt a _

RUNCORN RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL. The Grappenhall Sewerage Scheme. The fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Rural District Council was held at Dutton on Tuesday, Mr. C. E. Linaker presiding. THE REGULATION OF PROPERTY-BUILDINGd The Housing and Town Planning Committee reporte, = that tenders for the sewering of Wood's-row, Halton had been considered, and it was resolved that the tender of Messrs. J. G. Davies and Co.. amounting to £64 12s. tid., be accepted. A letter from certain owners and occupiers of property in Halton, asking the District Council to prevent the erection of cottage property on land adjoining the Frodsham waterworks reservoir, was read, and it was resolved that the owners be informed that the Council have no power to dictate to an owner of property as to the class of houses he shall erect thereon. The report was adopted. GRAPPENHALL AND ITS SEWERAGE. THREE SITES SUGGESTED FOR OUTFALL WORKS. The Grappenhall, Latchford Without and Thelwall Sewerage Committee met on Monday, when Mr. Pilling was appointed chairman. The engineer explained the scheme for flowering the three parishes, as prepared by him, and submitted at the Local Government Board inquiry. He also pointed out three alternative sites for sewerage disposal works. It was resolved that the respective owners of the sites pointed out by the engineer be approached for the terms upon which they would be prepared to let the Council have such quantity of land as may be necessary for sewerage disposal works ; that in the event of the Council being able to obtain any of such sites, the adjoining owners be informed and they be asked whether they would be pre- pared to support the Council in the event of them deciding upon its purchase for sewerage disposal works ; that, subject to satisfactory replies being obtained, the engineer prepare estimates of the cost of a scheme with outfall works on such sites as compared with the original scheme laid before the Local Govern- ment Board. A further meeting of the committee will be held at an early date. On the proposition of Mr. G. Pilling, the report was adopted.

SPORTS and PASTIMES.

FIRST DEFEAT. Warrington Fail Against Strong Eleven at Old Trafford. Warrington's unbeaten record was broken on Satur- day when they failed to accomplish the big task which was set them at Old Trafford. Manchester placed an exceptionally strong team, including several of the county professional staff, in the field. War- r ington, however, did not give a bad account of them- selves, indeed it was largely through an unlucky mis- chance that they were deprived of victory. W. Tyldesley, who passed the century for the home club, was missed at short leg at fifty, and there is little doubt that if the catch had been brcught off War- rington would have been triumphant. These little accidents will happen! The keenness of the match is evidenced by the fact that it was only on time that the Warrington score was passed. --- Warrington were first at the wicket, and before W Richardson and J. Bradburn were associated, they did not fare very promisingly. With these two players in, however, the prospects soon assumed a rosier com- plexion. The Warrington captain made 46 by stylish cricket and Bradburn put together a capital 42, his second highest ecore for Warrington. T. Wright made a characteristically vigorous 27, and G. Wood- cock, the old Botelerian who is on holiday from Cambridge University, knocked up 21. The last three batsmen— Hannam, A. Duckworth and W. Bennett— did not yield a single run between them, and gave J Tyldesley the opportunity of doing the hat trick. Tha. bowler was in good form and captured sixwickets fort 49 runs. The Manchester men owed their win to the notable innings of W. Tyldesley, who carried out his bat with 102 to his credit. The innings had realised 159 for eight wickets when play ceased. S. L. Partridge and Hannam did most of the bowling, capturing three wickets for 38 runs and three for 47 runs respectively. -- FIRST TEAM AVERAGES. BATTING. Not Aver- Inns. Out. Out. Runs. age. S. L. A. Bramhall was in tip-top form, and carried out his bat with 57 to his credit. Waywell and McGowan once more shared the bowling honours. -- Whitecross have withdrawn from the League, which now consists of half-a-dozen clubs. The following is the table to date : P. W. L. D. Pts. Five shilling pool, four prizes : Grant (Burtonwood), 1059; Lowe and Lowe 1,056; Hooper aud Son (Warrington) ; Hayward (Lowton), 1048. A NOTABLE SUCCESS. J. B. W. writes : I recently visited one of our local lofts and was surprised and pleased to know that Mr. J. Johnson, Howley Hotel, hed timed in a bird flown from Jersey for the Earlestown Amalgamation. The birds were liberated at 5.30, Mr. Johnson's bird homing to his loft at 2.30. The bird was a blue chequer, bred by Messrs. Jackson and Goulden, Penketh, and was presented to Mr. Johnson, who is to be congratulated upon winning £65 in cash, Mr. Longton's silver cup, and a five guinea oil-painting, while compating against all the Warrington and Earlestown cracks, who each flew two birds specially prepared for ,the race. Mr. Johnson attributed his success to the fact that he feeds his birds on corn supplied by Carter and Son, Warrington, and all werelin the pink of condition. Mr. Johnson has a good team. of cross-Channel birds for the Nantes race, and we all wish him every success on the forthcoming race, the Lancashire Nantes Combine. WATER POLO. Warrington's Easy Victory over Chester. There was a large number of spectators at the Corporation Baths on Thursday evening, when Warrington defeated Chester by eight goals to two in a polo match in connection with the Liverpool and District Water Polo and Squadron League division. The teams were :—Warrington : A. Jolley, goal; S. Ince and T. Pemberton, backs; J. Peers half-back; R. Pepper, H. Sisson and C. Turner, forwards. Cheater: G. Chrimes, goal ' • G. Gerrard and H. Kirkham, backs ; H. Hughes, half-back • A. Chadwick, F'. Dutton and L. Calvert, forwards. Mr. L. Golan, Birkenhead, was the referee. THE GAME. The Warrington spectators were somewhat amazed when the Chester forwards, playing from the shallow end, rushed straight up from the start, secured the ball and after a spicy combination, Dutton sent it crashing into the net. Thus rebuffed, Warrington tried hard, and Chrimes was repeatedly tested. From a corner Peers just missed scoring, but Chrimes sent the ball right out into the hand of Pepper, who equalised the sccres with a beautiful shot. 'There was no further score up to the interval, which arrived with the teams on equal terms. On resuming, Warrington were the only team in it. After a few seconds' play Pepper sent in e shot which, to all appearances, went through, but the referee decided against Warrington. Undaunted, the locals made a splendid effort, and Peers, who received on the left, netted the second goal for Warrington. Pepper soon added another, and, after just missing the sticks by inches, he recorded the fourth for Warrington. Gerrard scored for Chester with a rice shot, but from now to the finish it was only a matter of the Warring- ton forwards going up and petting the ball through. With the exception of Curimes, the Chester defence was very poor, and further goals were scored for War- rington by Peers, Pemberton, Turner and Ince. Sisson, who worked baru in the numerous raids, had hard lines on several occasions. The final came with the score : Warriegton, eight goals ; Chester, two. CLUB HANDICAP. The following were the heat winners in a5O yards club handicap in which there were 24 entries :—Messrs. E. Miller, T. Chantler, J. Wainwright and H. Silk. The final, which was very exciting, resulted as follows :- 1, Chantler ; 2, Miller ; 3, Silk.

FOOTBALL. A Great Chance for Warrington Schoolboys. A LANCASHIRE LEAGUE. The annual general meeting of the Lancashire County Football Union was held at the Grosvenor Hotel, Man- chester, on Tuesday evening. Clubs in membership are entitled to send one representative, exclusive of the member of the County Committee. Warrington was represented by Messrs. E. Osborne and T. Barnes. The HoN. Secretary (Mr. J. H. Houghton) in sub- mitting his report stated that the county team had not played up to their reputation, but some of the clubs had shown form worthy of the best traditions of the game. The Wigan Club had again carried off the Senior Cup, whilst the Pemberton Rovers won the Junior Cup in no uncertain manner. The S. Helens Club had won the Lancashire Shield for the first time after a keen game with the Widnes team at Warrington. Junior football had made splendid progress throughout the county, and he anticipated the future with much confidence. THE SHIELD FINAL AT WARRINGTON. The Hon. Treasurer (Mr. J. H. Smith) presented the statement of accounts for the season. The balance brought forward was £232 16s. 3d., and the amount in hand at the end of the season was £l5B 3s. Bd. It is interesting to note that the Shield Final at Warrington realised £4l 4s. Bd., and that a sum of £l3 Bs. 11d, was handed over to the British Playing Fields Society. Additions and alterations were made to the County bye-laws, Senior Cup rules, Junior Cup rules and the Lancashire Shield Competition. MEMBERS TO PAY. The most important alteration was that to the Senior Cup rules, which now makes it imperative for all mem- bers and ticket-holders to pay the admission cnarges to ground and stands for all cup-ties. Warrington opposed the alteration, but it was carried by a large majority. NEW COMMITTEE. Messrs. W. M. Gabbatt (Barrow), 11. Rebbitt (Broughton Rangers), R.Gale (Leigh), J. Valentine (Manchester and District League), J. Platt (Oldham), G. F. Hutchins (Oldham and District League), R. Collinge (Rochdale Hornets), H. Yates (Runcorn), J. Gough (Salford), J. H. Houghton (St. Helens), J. W. Scholes (Swinton), E. Osborne (Warrington), J. H. Smith (Widnes), J. Coansell (Wigan) were elected members of the committee for the season 1913-14. OFFICERS. At a meeting of the committee the following officers were elected for next season: Mr. R. Coiling° (Roch- dale), president ; J. Platt (Oldham), vice-president ; J. 11. Smith (Widnes), hon. treasurer; and J. H. Houghton (St. Helens), hon. secretary. NORTHERN RUGBY LEAGUE. At a meeting of the Northern!Rugby Football League Committee, the following officials were re-elected : President, J. B. Cooke ; vice-president, W. H. Cawley ; hon, treasurer, J. Platt; hon. secretary, J. H. Houghton. The St. Ilelens Recs., Castleford, and Brighouse Rangers' application to become members of the Northern Rugby Combination were granted. Messrs. Cooke, Wood, and Houghton were elected to represent the League on the Referees' Board of Examination. West Lancashire League. Shield for Schoolboys' Competition. At a meeting of the League it was decided to abandon the competition and to offer the shield for a School- Boys' League to be formed of the clubs in West Lancashire. We understand that representatives from the various towns are to meet at an early date to formulate rules and to place the league on a proper footing. This is a step in the right direction, and we are sure the committee have acted wisely. Schoolboys are very keen enthusiasts, and the presentation of a shield for competition will do much to enhance the game and to create a friendly rivalry amongst the various clubs in each of the towns privileged to com- pete. The Warrington schoolboys have done well against other towns' boys. May they be the first to win the shield ! RINK HOCKEY. Works League Competition. SEASON 1913. P. W. L. D.
